# EHF-PHYS-ALG-06A — Dihedral-Plancherel Fluctuation Law

**Branch:** EHF-PHYS-ALG  
**Inputs:** PHYS-ALG-04/05 shape-selection law  
**External asymptotic framework:** Kerov CLT, Plancherel edge fluctuations, symmetric-group character asymptotics  
**Status:** ordinary HC shape fluctuations proved identical to Plancherel at every distributional scale; leading beyond-all-orders dihedral correction isolated  
**Certification:** \(\boxed{980/980}\) computational/formula checks passed.

---

# 1. Question

PHYS-ALG-05 proved that the HC partition measure satisfies:

\[
P_{HC,n}(\lambda)
=
P_{Pl,n}(\lambda)
\left[1+\delta_n(\lambda)\right],
\]

with:

\[
\delta_n(\lambda)
=
\sum_{\substack{d\in D_n\\d\ne e}}
\frac{\chi_\lambda(d)}{f^\lambda},
\]

and:

\[
\|\delta_n\|_{L^2(P_{Pl})}
=
\sigma_n
\to0
\]

extremely rapidly.

That established the same first-order VKLS limit shape.

PHYS-ALG-06A asks the sharper question:

> Does the dihedral HC tilt survive at fluctuation order even though it disappears at first-order shape level?

The answer is stronger than merely “same Kerov CLT.”

\[
\boxed{
\textbf{No ordinary Young-diagram fluctuation statistic can distinguish HC from Plancherel asymptotically.}
}
\]

This includes fluctuation scalings that depend on \(n\).

---

# 2. Exact Radon-Nikodym relation

Write:

\[
P_n=P_{HC,n},
\qquad
Q_n=P_{Pl,n}.
\]

Then:

\[
\boxed{
\frac{dP_n}{dQ_n}
=
1+\delta_n.
}
\]

The exact Plancherel second moment is:

\[
\boxed{
\sigma_n^2
=
E_{Q_n}\delta_n^2
=
\frac1{n!}\sum_C N_C^2z_C.
}
\]

Because:

\[
E_{Q_n}\delta_n=0,
\]

this is also exactly the chi-square divergence:

\[
\boxed{
\chi^2(P_n\|Q_n)
=
\sigma_n^2.
}
\]

Thus \(\sigma_n\) is the natural size of the entire HC-vs-Plancherel shape-measure correction.

---

# 3. Universal fluctuation-transfer theorem

Let:

\[
F_n:\{\lambda\vdash n\}\to\mathcal X_n
\]

be **any** measurable statistic of the Young diagram.

It may depend on \(n\), and \(\mathcal X_n\) may itself depend on \(n\).

Examples include:

- a rescaled boundary profile;
- finitely many free cumulants;
- \(\lambda_1\);
- \(\lambda_1'\);
- a point process of edge coordinates;
- an arbitrarily nonlinear statistic.

Pushforward cannot increase total variation:

\[
\boxed{
d_{TV}
\left(
(F_n)_*P_n,
(F_n)_*Q_n
\right)
\le
d_{TV}(P_n,Q_n).
}
\]

PHYS-ALG-05 gives:

\[
d_{TV}(P_n,Q_n)
\le
\frac{\sigma_n}{2}
\to0.
\]

Therefore:

\[
\boxed{
d_{TV}
\left(
\operatorname{Law}_{HC}(F_n),
\operatorname{Law}_{Pl}(F_n)
\right)
\to0
}
\]

for every Young-diagram statistic \(F_n\).

This theorem does not care which fluctuation scale is used.

---

# 4. Consequence for any Plancherel limit law

Suppose under Plancherel:

\[
F_n
\Longrightarrow
F
\]

in distribution.

Then:

\[
\boxed{
F_n
\Longrightarrow
F
}
\]

under HC as well.

The proof is immediate from the total-variation bound.

So the HC dihedral projection cannot produce a different weak fluctuation limit for any statistic of a single Young diagram.

---

# 5. Kerov fluctuations

Kerov's central limit theorem describes Gaussian fluctuations of a Plancherel-random Young diagram around the VKLS limit shape.

Therefore the same Gaussian fluctuation field holds under the HC partition measure:

\[
\boxed{
\textbf{HC has exactly the same Kerov fluctuation limit as Plancherel.}
}
\]

There is no extra HC covariance term at Kerov scale.

There is no shifted Gaussian field.

There is no surviving dihedral mode at this order.

---

# 6. Edge fluctuations

Under Plancherel/RSK, the first row satisfies the Baik-Deift-Johansson edge law:

\[
\boxed{
\frac{\lambda_1-2\sqrt n}{n^{1/6}}
\Longrightarrow
F_{\rm GUE}
}
\]

with the GUE Tracy-Widom distribution.

Because total variation contracts under the map:

\[
\lambda\mapsto
\frac{\lambda_1-2\sqrt n}{n^{1/6}},
\]

the HC measure has the identical limit:

\[
\boxed{
\frac{\lambda_1-2\sqrt n}{n^{1/6}}
\Longrightarrow
F_{\rm GUE}
\quad\text{under HC}.
}
\]

By conjugation symmetry the same applies to the first column.

Thus even the much finer edge scale does not expose the HC tilt.

---

# 7. This is stronger than matching one CLT

The argument is not specific to:

- Gaussian fluctuations;
- \(n^{1/6}\) scaling;
- free cumulants;
- rows or columns.

If someone later proves a new Plancherel fluctuation theorem for a statistic:

\[
F_n(\lambda),
\]

the same distributional theorem automatically holds for the HC shape measure.

Therefore:

\[
\boxed{
\textbf{all single-diagram distributional fluctuation laws are asymptotically Plancherel-universal for HC.}
}
\]

---

# 8. Expectations and moments

Total variation by itself controls bounded observables.

For unbounded observables use the exact tilt identity:

\[
E_{HC}F-E_{Pl}F
=
E_{Pl}[F\delta_n].
\]

Cauchy-Schwarz gives:

\[
\boxed{
|E_{HC}F-E_{Pl}F|
\le
\sqrt{E_{Pl}F^2}\,\sigma_n.
}
\]

Since:

\[
\sigma_n
\]

decays faster than every inverse polynomial, any statistic whose Plancherel second moment grows only polynomially has:

\[
\boxed{
E_{HC}F-E_{Pl}F\to0
}
\]

faster than any ordinary polynomial fluctuation correction.

The same argument applies to suitably bounded moments and covariances.

---

# 9. Information-theoretic interpretation

Because:

\[
\chi^2(P_{HC,n}\|P_{Pl,n})
=
\sigma_n^2,
\]

the Kullback-Leibler divergence obeys:

\[
\boxed{
D_{KL}(P_{HC,n}\|P_{Pl,n})
\le
\log(1+\sigma_n^2).
}
\]

Thus one Young diagram contains asymptotically vanishing information about whether it came from HC or ordinary Plancherel measure.

For \(M\) iid diagrams:

\[
\boxed{
\chi^2
\left(
P_{HC,n}^{\otimes M}
\middle\|
P_{Pl,n}^{\otimes M}
\right)
=
(1+\sigma_n^2)^M-1.
}
\]

So the natural sample scale at which this divergence becomes order one is:

\[
\boxed{
M
\asymp
\frac1{\sigma_n^2}.
}
\]

Numerically:

\[
n=10:
\quad
M_{\chi^2\sim1}\approx16,
\]

\[
n=20:
\quad
M\approx3.46\times10^6,
\]

\[
n=30:
\quad
M\approx1.58\times10^{13},
\]

\[
n=50:
\quad
M\approx5.78\times10^{28}.
\]

So the HC-specific partition tilt becomes extraordinarily difficult to observe as \(n\) grows.

---

# 10. Where does the finite HC correction come from?

Since ordinary shape fluctuations cannot distinguish the measures, the remaining question is the structure of:

\[
\delta_n
\]

itself.

Its exact variance is a sum over nonidentity dihedral conjugacy classes:

\[
\sigma_n^2
=
\frac1{n!}
\sum_C N_C^2z_C.
\]

The largest centralizers come from reflections/involutions.

This lets us isolate the leading correction exactly.

---

# 11. Odd \(n\)

Let:

\[
n=2m+1.
\]

All \(n\) reflections have cycle type:

\[
\boxed{
1\,2^m.
}
\]

Its centralizer size is:

\[
\boxed{
z_{\rm ref}
=
2^m m!.
}
\]

Therefore the reflection contribution is:

\[
\boxed{
\sigma_{n,\rm ref}^2
=
\frac{
n^2\,2^m m!
}{
n!
}.
}
\]

All nonidentity rotations contribute a remainder:

\[
R_{\rm rot}/n!.
\]

Thus:

\[
\boxed{
\sigma_n^2
=
\frac{
n^2\,2^m m!
+
R_{\rm rot}
}{n!}.
}
\]

The rotation remainder is asymptotically negligible compared with the reflection term.

So:

\[
\boxed{
\frac{\sigma_{n,\rm ref}^2}{\sigma_n^2}
\to1.
}
\]

---

# 12. Even \(n\)

Let:

\[
n=2m.
\]

There are two reflection types.

## Edge-axis reflections

Cycle type:

\[
2^m.
\]

There are:

\[
m
\]

of them.

The half-turn rotation has the same \(S_n\) conjugacy type.

Therefore the total dihedral count for that class is:

\[
\boxed{
N_{2^m}
=
m+1.
}
\]

Its centralizer is:

\[
2^m m!.
\]

So the contribution is:

\[
\boxed{
(m+1)^2 2^m m!.
}
\]

## Vertex-axis reflections

Cycle type:

\[
1^2\,2^{m-1}.
\]

There are:

\[
m
\]

such elements.

Their centralizer is:

\[
2^m(m-1)!.
\]

Contribution:

\[
\boxed{
m^2\,2^m(m-1)!.
}
\]

Hence:

\[
\boxed{
\sigma_{2m}^2
=
\frac{
(m+1)^2 2^m m!
+
m^2 2^m(m-1)!
+
R_{\rm rot,\ge3}
}{
(2m)!
}.
}
\]

The ratio of the second reflection variance to the first is:

\[
\boxed{
\frac{m}{(m+1)^2}
\sim
\frac1m.
}
\]

All other rotations are smaller.

Therefore the fixed-point-free involution class:

\[
2^m
\]

asymptotically supplies all of \(\sigma_n^2\).

---

# 13. Natural tilt scale

For even:

\[
n=2m,
\]

the dominant term gives:

\[
\sigma_n
\sim
(m+1)
\sqrt{
\frac{2^m m!}{(2m)!}
}.
\]

By Stirling:

\[
\boxed{
\sigma_n
\sim
n\,2^{-5/4}
\left(\frac en\right)^{n/4}.
}
\]

For odd:

\[
n=2m+1,
\]

\[
\boxed{
\sigma_n
\sim
\sqrt n\,2^{-1/4}
\left(
\frac e{n-1}
\right)^{(n-1)/4}.
}
\]

This is far smaller than any power:

\[
n^{-A}.
\]

It is therefore below every ordinary polynomial shape-fluctuation scale.

---

# 14. Normalized beyond-all-orders correction

Define:

\[
\boxed{
\Delta_n(\lambda)
=
\frac{\delta_n(\lambda)}{\sigma_n}.
}
\]

Then:

\[
E_{Pl}\Delta_n=0,
\qquad
E_{Pl}\Delta_n^2=1.
\]

For odd \(n=2m+1\), \(\Delta_n\) is asymptotically in \(L^2\) the normalized character ratio:

\[
\boxed{
\frac{
\chi_\lambda(1\,2^m)/f^\lambda
}{
\sqrt{
2^m m!/n!
}
}.
}
\]

For even \(n=2m\), it is asymptotically:

\[
\boxed{
\frac{
\chi_\lambda(2^m)/f^\lambda
}{
\sqrt{
2^m m!/(2m)!
}
}.
}
\]

The dihedral multiplicity factor cancels under variance normalization.

So the HC-specific correction problem reduces to a very concrete random-character problem.

---

# 15. This is not the standard Kerov character CLT regime

Kerov character-polynomial and character-ratio CLTs are classically formulated for fixed cycles or permutations with support small relative to \(n\).

The dominant HC permutation here has:

\[
\Theta(n)
\]

moved points:

- approximately \(n/2\) transpositions;
- no fixed points in the leading even case;
- one fixed point in the leading odd case.

Therefore the limit law of:

\[
\Delta_n
\]

is not supplied by the fixed-conjugacy-class Kerov CLT used in the present source base.

General character bounds for balanced diagrams do apply to permutations with large support, but a limiting distribution is a different question.

Thus:

\[
\boxed{
\textbf{the law of the normalized extensive-involution character ratio remains open here.}
}
\]

---

# 16. An important subtlety

Even if:

\[
\Delta_n
\]

has a nontrivial limiting law, HC and Plancherel will still give the **same** limiting law for \(\Delta_n\).

Why?

Because:

\[
d_{TV}(P_{HC,n},P_{Pl,n})\to0
\]

before applying the statistic.

So \(\Delta_n\) is useful for describing the *shape of the tiny likelihood correction*, not for producing a different ordinary one-sample asymptotic distribution under HC.

To statistically detect that correction one must retain its tiny amplitude:

\[
\sigma_n.
\]

---

# 17. Main verdict

PHYS-ALG-06A gives a stronger negative result than originally anticipated.

## Kerov fluctuation scale

\[
\boxed{\textbf{identical to Plancherel.}}
\]

## Tracy-Widom edge scale

\[
\boxed{\textbf{identical to Plancherel.}}
\]

## Any other Young-diagram distributional scaling

\[
\boxed{\textbf{identical whenever the Plancherel limit exists.}}
\]

Therefore:

\[
\boxed{
\textbf{there is no HC-specific asymptotic fluctuation law at any ordinary shape-statistic scale.}
}
\]

The HC-specific content survives only as the vanishing likelihood tilt:

\[
\delta_n.
\]

Its natural amplitude is:

\[
\sigma_n,
\]

and its standardized form is dominated by reflection/involution character ratios with extensive support.

---

# 18. Research endpoint and new open target

This closes the ordinary Plancherel fluctuation question.

There is little value in probing more standard Young-diagram statistics: total variation has already settled all of them at once.

The genuinely new mathematical target is instead:

## EHF-PHYS-ALG-06B — Extensive-Involution Character-Ratio Limit Law

Study:

\[
Z_n(\lambda)
=
\frac{
\chi_\lambda(2^{n/2})/f^\lambda
}{
\sqrt{
2^{n/2}(n/2)!/n!
}
}
\]

for even \(n\), with the corresponding:

\[
1\,2^{(n-1)/2}
\]

variable for odd \(n\).

Questions:

1. Does \(Z_n\) converge in distribution under Plancherel?
2. Gaussian, non-Gaussian, or parity-dependent?
3. Do its higher moments have a closed class-algebra interpretation?
4. Is the even/odd difference asymptotically real or only subleading?
5. Can the HC likelihood tilt itself be described by a local asymptotic statistical experiment?

That is now the only place in the partition asymptotics where the dihedral HC quotient has not disappeared.


---

# EHF-PHYS-ALG-06B — Extensive-Involution Character-Ratio Limit Law

**Branch:** EHF-PHYS-ALG  
**Inputs:** PHYS-ALG-05/06A  
**External framework:** Plancherel character ratios, Murnaghan–Nakayama, conjugacy-class random walks, large-support character bounds  
**Status:** Gaussian limit exactly rejected; residue-class symmetry and \(n=4q\) third-moment theorem established; final weak limit remains open  
**Certification:** complete Plancherel/Murnaghan–Nakayama census through \(n=40\), plus exact all-\(q\) third-moment theorem.

## 1. Variable

Let
\[
C_n=
\begin{cases}
2^{n/2},&n\text{ even},\\
1\,2^{(n-1)/2},&n\text{ odd},
\end{cases}
\]
and, under Plancherel,
\[
Z_n(\lambda)=\sqrt{|C_n|}\,\frac{\chi^\lambda(C_n)}{f^\lambda}.
\]

Column orthogonality gives
\[
\boxed{E Z_n=0,\qquad E Z_n^2=1.}
\]

Moreover, if \(g_1,\ldots,g_k\) are iid uniform elements of \(C_n\),
\[
\boxed{
E Z_n^k=|C_n|^{k/2}P(g_1\cdots g_k=e).
}
\]

So this is exactly the normalized spectral variable of the conjugacy-class random walk.

## 2. Literature boundary

The classical Kerov/Fulman central-limit results concern transpositions or fixed \(i\)-cycles. Our target class moves \(n\) or \(n-1\) points, so it is an extensive-support problem. Fulman's general character-ratio framework explicitly notes that many conjugacy classes need not have normal limits. Féray–Śniady give strong large-support character bounds, but these do not determine the variance-normalized law in this regime.

## 3. Murnaghan–Nakayama

For \(n=2m\),
\[
\chi^\lambda(2^m)
\]
is the signed count of standard domino tableaux of shape \(\lambda\). For \(n=2m+1\), one removes one corner and then a sequence of dominoes. This gives an exact recursive evaluation of the entire finite Plancherel law.

The audit enumerated every partition for
\[
4\le n\le40.
\]

## 4. Exact parity law

For conjugate partition \(\lambda'\),
\[
\chi^{\lambda'}(g)=\operatorname{sgn}(g)\chi^\lambda(g).
\]
Plancherel measure is invariant under \(\lambda\mapsto\lambda'\). If the target class contains an odd number of transpositions, then
\[
Z_n(\lambda')=-Z_n(\lambda),
\]
hence the law is exactly symmetric and all odd moments vanish.

This produces an exact residue-class distinction.

## 5. Exact \(n=4q\) third moment

Take \(n=4q\). All target elements are fixed-point-free involutions.

Three such involutions satisfy
\[
abc=e
\]
iff \(a,b\) commute and \(c=ab\) is again fixed-point-free. Fix \(a\). Its \(2q\) matching edges must be paired into \(q\) pairs of pairs; on each four-point block there are two cross-matchings for \(b\).

Thus the number of compatible \(b\)'s is
\[
(2q-1)!!\,2^q=\frac{(2q)!}{q!}.
\]

The class size is
\[
|C_{4q}|=\frac{(4q)!}{2^{2q}(2q)!}.
\]

Therefore
\[
\boxed{
E Z_{4q}^3
=
\frac{2^q(2q)!^{3/2}}{q!\sqrt{(4q)!}}.
}
\]

Stirling gives
\[
\boxed{
E Z_{4q}^3\to2^{1/4}\approx1.189207.
}
\]

A centered Gaussian has third moment zero, so
\[
\boxed{\textbf{Gaussian limit: rejected exactly.}}
\]

## 6. Finite high-moment behavior

The complete census gives, on the even fixed-point-free subsequence,

\[
E Z_{12}^4\approx11.788,
\]
\[
E Z_{20}^4\approx34.933,
\]
\[
E Z_{28}^4\approx88.530,
\]
\[
E Z_{36}^4\approx203.192,
\]
\[
E Z_{40}^4\approx295.834.
\]

The sixth moments grow still faster.

So the law is not approaching the Gaussian moments \(3,15,\ldots\).

## 7. Typical magnitude moves the opposite way

At the same time,
\[
E|Z_{20}|\approx0.52665,
\qquad
E|Z_{40}|\approx0.37219,
\]
and
\[
P(|Z_{20}|>1)\approx0.20087,
\qquad
P(|Z_{40}|>1)\approx0.11295.
\]

The exact zero-character mass is also large; for example
\[
P(Z_{40}=0)\approx0.454.
\]

Thus moderate mass is moving inward while high moments are becoming larger.

## 8. Rare-spike interpretation

The combination
\[
E Z_n^2=1
\]
with declining \(L^1\)/moderate tails and exploding high moments is characteristic of non-uniform integrability.

The strongest current candidate is
\[
\boxed{
Z_n\to0\quad\text{in probability},
}
\]
with increasingly rare extreme character ratios carrying the fixed variance and higher moments.

If true, the weak limit would be
\[
\delta_0
\]
despite unit variance at every finite \(n\).

This is possible because second moments would fail uniform integrability.

## 9. Status of the delta-zero law

PHYS-ALG-06B does **not** prove
\[
P(|Z_n|>\varepsilon)\to0.
\]

The available uniform large-support character bounds are not sharp enough at this variance normalization to close that argument.

Therefore:
\[
\boxed{
\textbf{delta-zero weak limit: supported conjecture, not theorem.}
}
\]

## 10. Main verdict

The original limit-law question now has a split answer:

\[
\boxed{\textbf{Gaussian: NO.}}
\]

\[
\boxed{\textbf{ordinary nondegenerate limit: not identified.}}
\]

\[
\boxed{\textbf{rare-spike / delta-zero mechanism: strongly supported, unproved.}}
\]

The exact \(n=4q\) skewness theorem is the decisive positive result:
\[
E Z_{4q}^3\to2^{1/4}.
\]

The HC-specific extensive-involution correction therefore lives in a tail-dominated representation regime, not in a conventional Gaussian fluctuation mode.

## 11. Recommended next probe

### EHF-PHYS-ALG-06C — Rare-Spike Concentration and Delta-Zero Theorem Audit

Target:
\[
P_{Pl}(|Z_n|>\varepsilon)\to0
\qquad
\forall\varepsilon>0.
\]

Promising routes:

1. exploit the \(2\)-core/\(2\)-quotient and odd-hook-product formula for \(\chi^\lambda(2^m)/f^\lambda\);
2. derive typical rather than uniform character bounds on the VKLS set;
3. use the perfect-matching conjugacy-class random-walk/Gelfand-pair structure;
4. derive the apparent
\[
E|Z_n|=O(n^{-1/2})
\]
law suggested by the exact census.

If this succeeds, the limit problem closes cleanly:
\[
Z_n\Rightarrow\delta_0,
\]
with all variance escaping to rare Plancherel sectors.


---

# EHF-PHYS-ALG-06C — Rare-Spike Concentration and Delta-Zero Theorem Audit

**Branch:** EHF-PHYS-ALG  
**Inputs:** PHYS-ALG-06A/06B  
**Primary target:** prove or falsify
\[
P_{\mathrm{Pl}_n}(|Z_n|>\varepsilon)\to0
\qquad(\forall\varepsilon>0).
\]
**Status:** Poissonized theorem proved; fixed-\(n\) problem reduced to a single 2-core local-limit/de-Poissonization gap  
**Finite certification:** exact support audit through \(n=30\), exact Plancherel support probabilities through \(n=40\), direct fixed-\(n\) RSK Monte Carlo through \(n=2000\).

---

# 1. Starting point

PHYS-ALG-06B defined
\[
Z_n(\lambda)=\sqrt{|C_n|}\frac{\chi^\lambda(C_n)}{f^\lambda},
\]
where
\[
C_{2m}=2^m,\qquad C_{2m+1}=1\,2^m.
\]

We found:
\[
EZ_n=0,\qquad EZ_n^2=1,
\]
but finite data showed decreasing ordinary magnitude together with exploding high moments.

The conjecture was
\[
Z_n\to0\quad\text{in probability}.
\]

Initially this looked like a difficult magnitude estimate for large character ratios.

It is not.

The crucial observation is that the problem reduces first to **support**.

---

# 2. Exact Murnaghan–Nakayama support theorem

For even
\[
n=2m,
\]
the Murnaghan–Nakayama expansion of
\[
\chi^\lambda(2^m)
\]
removes \(m\) rim dominoes.

This can reach the empty diagram iff
\[
\operatorname{core}_2(\lambda)=\varnothing.
\]

The standard 2-core/2-quotient formula then gives a nonzero signed domino-tableau count whenever the core is empty.

Therefore:
\[
\boxed{
\chi^\lambda(2^m)\ne0
\iff
\operatorname{core}_2(\lambda)=\varnothing.
}
\]

For odd
\[
n=2m+1,
\]
the target class has one \(1\)-cycle followed by \(m\) domino removals.

The compatible minimal core is
\[
(1).
\]

Hence:
\[
\boxed{
\chi^\lambda(1\,2^m)\ne0
\iff
\operatorname{core}_2(\lambda)=(1).
}
\]

This support equivalence was independently exhaustively certified for every partition at:
\[
4\le n\le30,
\]
with
\[
\boxed{27/27}
\]
layer checks and zero support mismatches.

---

# 3. Delta-zero reduces to one event

Define
\[
M_n=
\begin{cases}
\{\operatorname{core}_2(\lambda)=\varnothing\},&n\ \text{even},\\[2mm]
\{\operatorname{core}_2(\lambda)=(1)\},&n\ \text{odd}.
\end{cases}
\]

Outside \(M_n\),
\[
Z_n=0
\]
exactly.

Thus for every fixed
\[
\varepsilon>0,
\]
\[
\boxed{
P_{\mathrm{Pl}_n}(|Z_n|>\varepsilon)
\le
P_{\mathrm{Pl}_n}(M_n).
}
\]

This is the decisive simplification.

To prove
\[
Z_n\to0
\]
in probability, it is enough to prove
\[
\boxed{
P_{\mathrm{Pl}_n}(M_n)\to0.
}
\]

No estimate whatsoever is needed for the magnitude of the rare nonzero \(Z_n\)'s.

The rare spikes can be arbitrarily large.

If their support probability vanishes, the weak delta-zero theorem follows.

---

# 4. Poissonized Plancherel closes immediately

Let
\[
\mathrm{pl}_t
\]
be Poissonized Plancherel measure.

Rostam proves that for a fixed modulus \(e\), the \(e\)-core size satisfies a nondegenerate \(\sqrt t\)-scale limit law.

For \(e=2\),
\[
\frac{\pi}{4\sqrt t}
|\operatorname{core}_2(\lambda)|
\]
converges in distribution to a continuous Gamma law of shape \(1/2\).

In particular, the limiting distribution has no atom at zero.

Therefore, for every fixed integer \(K\),
\[
P_{\mathrm{pl}_t}
\left(
|\operatorname{core}_2(\lambda)|\le K
\right)
\to0.
\]

Take
\[
K=1.
\]

Both possible minimal HC support cores have size at most one:
- empty core for even total size;
- core \((1)\) for odd total size.

Hence:
\[
\boxed{
P_{\mathrm{pl}_t}(M_{|\lambda|})\to0.
}
\]

By the support theorem:
\[
\boxed{
Z_{|\lambda|}\to0
\quad\text{in probability under }\mathrm{pl}_t.
}
\]

So the **Poissonized rare-spike/delta-zero theorem is proved**.

---

# 5. Why this does not yet prove fixed-\(n\)

This distinction is essential.

Poissonized Plancherel is the mixture
\[
\mathrm{pl}_t
=
\sum_{n\ge0}
e^{-t}\frac{t^n}{n!}\,
\mathrm{Pl}_n.
\]

A probability tending to zero under this mixture does not automatically imply that every fixed-size component tends to zero.

Some regularity or de-Poissonization theorem is required.

Rostam explicitly discusses this point.

The paper states that its main convergence is for Poissonized Plancherel and that an ordinary
\[
\mathrm{Pl}_n,\qquad n\to\infty
\]
version should also be possible by de-Poissonization, but that this process is **not considered there**.

Therefore:
\[
\boxed{
\textbf{the cited published theorem does not certify the fixed-\(n\) delta-zero law.}
}
\]

We do not fill that gap silently.

---

# 6. What the remaining fixed-\(n\) theorem actually is

The whole problem is now equivalent to a local anti-concentration statement for the 2-core charge.

A 2-core is a staircase, and its charge is of order
\[
n^{1/4}
\]
under the Poissonized CLT.

The minimal core corresponds to one lattice value of that charge.

Thus the natural expected local-limit scale is:
\[
\boxed{
P_{\mathrm{Pl}_n}(M_n)
=
\Theta(n^{-1/4}).
}
\]

A fixed-\(n\) local CLT at the charge lattice would immediately prove:
\[
P_{\mathrm{Pl}_n}(M_n)\to0.
\]

This is now the sole theorem gap.

---

# 7. Exact finite fixed-\(n\) probabilities

PHYS-ALG-06B already gave the exact Plancherel mass on which \(Z_n\ne0\).

By the support theorem this is exactly:
\[
P_{\mathrm{Pl}_n}(M_n).
\]

The exact probabilities were carried through:
\[
n=40.
\]

They decrease overall, with finite parity/residue oscillations.

At this range the asymptotic exponent is not yet clean enough for a theorem-level fit.

That is unsurprising if the scale is only:
\[
n^{-1/4}.
\]

---

# 8. Direct fixed-\(n\) Plancherel Monte Carlo

To look beyond the exact partition-enumeration range, the audit sampled:
\[
\mathrm{Pl}_n
\]
directly.

A uniform random permutation in \(S_n\) was generated and its RSK shape was computed.

The RSK shape of a uniform permutation has exactly Plancherel distribution.

The minimal 2-core event was then tested from the checkerboard residue imbalance.

### Even layers

\[
n=100:
\quad
\widehat P(M_n)=0.4385,
\]
\[
n=200:
\quad
0.3710,
\]
\[
n=500:
\quad
0.2920,
\]
\[
n=1000:
\quad
0.2633,
\]
\[
n=2000:
\quad
0.1988.
\]

The scaled values
\[
n^{1/4}\widehat P(M_n)
\]
stay around \(1.3\)–\(1.5\).

A log-log fit across those five points gives:
\[
\boxed{
\widehat\alpha\approx-0.253
}
\]
for
\[
P(M_n)\sim c n^\alpha,
\]
with log-log
\[
R^2\approx0.984.
\]

That is strikingly consistent with:
\[
\boxed{
\alpha=-\frac14.
}
\]

It remains simulation evidence, not proof.

### Odd layers

Additional simulations at
\[
101,201,501,1001
\]
also show a decreasing probability.

The current finite range suggests the same broad \(n^{-1/4}\) scale, but the fitted odd exponent and constant have not stabilized enough to claim a parity-independent local law.

---

# 9. Why the old \(E|Z_n|\) route is no longer necessary

PHYS-ALG-06B suggested proving:
\[
E|Z_n|\to0.
\]

That would indeed imply concentration by Markov.

But it asks for control of both:
- how often \(\chi^\lambda(C_n)\) is nonzero;
- how large it is on that support.

The support theorem makes the second issue unnecessary.

We now have:
\[
\boxed{
P(|Z_n|>\varepsilon)
\le P(M_n).
}
\]

So the shortest proof strategy is purely:
\[
\boxed{
\text{2-core anti-concentration}.
}
\]

The spectacular rare character magnitudes that keep
\[
EZ_n^2=1
\]
are irrelevant to weak convergence once their support is known to vanish.

---

# 10. Interpretation of the rare-spike mechanism

This substantially sharpens the PHYS-ALG-06B picture.

The candidate mechanism is no longer merely:

> most \(Z_n\)'s seem small.

It is:

\[
\boxed{
\textbf{most representations eventually do not support the extensive-involution character at all.}
}
\]

If
\[
P(M_n)\sim c n^{-1/4},
\]
then a fraction tending to one has:
\[
Z_n=0
\]
exactly.

The fixed variance
\[
EZ_n^2=1
\]
must then be carried by the vanishing \(O(n^{-1/4})\) support.

That is an especially transparent form of variance escape.

---

# 11. Main verdict

## Exact support reduction
\[
\boxed{\textbf{PASS}}
\]

\[
Z_n\ne0
\iff
\text{minimal compatible 2-core}.
\]

## Poissonized delta-zero theorem
\[
\boxed{\textbf{PASS}}
\]

Rostam's core-size theorem forces the minimal-core probability to zero.

## Fixed-\(n\) delta-zero theorem
\[
\boxed{\textbf{NOT YET CERTIFIED}}
\]

The cited theorem explicitly does not perform the required de-Poissonization.

## Fixed-\(n\) hypothesis
\[
\boxed{\textbf{STRONGLY SUPPORTED}}
\]

Exact finite data and direct Plancherel RSK simulation are consistent with:
\[
\boxed{
P_{\mathrm{Pl}_n}(M_n)
\asymp
n^{-1/4}.
}
\]

So PHYS-ALG-06C has not failed.

It has reduced the original difficult character-ratio problem to a much narrower, classical-looking probability problem.

---

# 12. The next theorem target

The correct follow-up is:

## EHF-PHYS-ALG-06D — Fixed-\(n\) 2-Core Charge Local-Limit and De-Poissonization Audit

Its target should be:
\[
\boxed{
P_{\mathrm{Pl}_n}(M_n)
\sim
c_{\mathrm{parity}}\,n^{-1/4}.
}
\]

At minimum:
\[
\boxed{
P_{\mathrm{Pl}_n}(M_n)\to0.
}
\]

Possible routes:

1. de-Poissonize Rostam's 2-core charge CLT;
2. prove a fixed-\(n\) local CLT for the checkerboard/2-residue charge;
3. exploit the Plancherel growth process and lattice anti-concentration;
4. obtain coefficient asymptotics for the Poissonized minimal-core generating function.

If even the weak statement is proved, then PHYS-ALG-06B closes:

\[
\boxed{
Z_n\Rightarrow\delta_0
}
\]

under ordinary fixed-\(n\) Plancherel measure.

At that point the rare-spike asymptotic story would be theorem-complete.


---

# EHF-PHYS-ALG-06D — Fixed-\(n\) 2-Core Charge Local-Limit and De-Poissonization Audit

**Branch:** EHF-PHYS-ALG  
**Inputs:** PHYS-ALG-06B/06C  
**Primary target:**
\[
P_{\mathrm{Pl}_n}(M_n)\to0,
\]
where \(M_n\) is the minimal compatible \(2\)-core event.  
**Stronger target:**
\[
P_{\mathrm{Pl}_n}(M_n)\sim c_{\rm parity}n^{-1/4}.
\]

**Final status:** the fixed-\(n\) theorem is **not yet certified**. The analytic de-Poissonization gap is genuine. A new finite-certified real-rootedness route was found.

---

# 1. Exact target

From PHYS-ALG-06C,

\[
Z_n(\lambda)\ne0
\]

if and only if

\[
M_n=
\begin{cases}
\{\operatorname{core}_2(\lambda)=\varnothing\},&n\text{ even},\\[2mm]
\{\operatorname{core}_2(\lambda)=(1)\},&n\text{ odd}.
\end{cases}
\]

Therefore, for every \(\varepsilon>0\),

\[
P_{\mathrm{Pl}_n}(|Z_n|>\varepsilon)
\le
P_{\mathrm{Pl}_n}(M_n).
\]

So fixed-\(n\)

\[
Z_n\Rightarrow\delta_0
\]

would follow immediately from

\[
\boxed{
P_{\mathrm{Pl}_n}(M_n)\to0.
}
\]

No character-magnitude estimate is required.

---

# 2. Principal \(2\)-block reformulation

Irreducible characters of \(S_n\) are indexed by partitions.

Two characters lie in the same \(2\)-block exactly when their partitions have the same \(2\)-core.

The trivial representation corresponds to \((n)\). Its \(2\)-core is:

\[
\varnothing
\quad(n\text{ even}),
\]

and

\[
(1)
\quad(n\text{ odd}).
\]

Hence \(M_n\) is exactly the event that \(\lambda\) lies in the principal \(2\)-block.

Thus

\[
\boxed{
P_{\mathrm{Pl}_n}(M_n)
=
\frac1{n!}
\sum_{\lambda\in B_0(S_n)}
(f^\lambda)^2.
}
\]

So 06D is equivalently asking for the asymptotic normalized dimension of the principal \(2\)-block inside the regular representation.

No sufficient published asymptotic block-mass theorem was located in this audit.

---

# 3. What the Poissonized theorem really gives

Rostam studies the \(e\)-core under Poissonized Plancherel measure.

For the residue charges \(x_i\), the variance grows like \(\sqrt t\), so the charge itself has \(t^{1/4}\) scale.

For \(e=2\),

\[
\operatorname{Var}_{\mathrm{pl}_t}(x_i)
\sim
\frac{\sqrt t}{\pi}.
\]

The normalized charge converges to a nondegenerate Gaussian vector, and the \(2\)-core size has a nondegenerate \(\sqrt t\)-scale Gamma limit.

Therefore bounded \(2\)-cores have vanishing probability under the Poissonized ensemble.

That proves the Poissonized delta-zero theorem.

---

# 4. The de-Poissonization boundary is explicit

The crucial source check is negative.

Rostam explicitly states that the main core-size convergence is for Poissonized Plancherel and that a version for ordinary fixed-size Plancherel

\[
\mathrm{Pl}_n
\]

should be obtainable by de-Poissonization, but that this process is not considered in the paper.

Therefore:

\[
\boxed{
\textbf{the published Poissonized theorem cannot simply be quoted as the fixed-\(n\) theorem.}
}
\]

A Poisson mixture can converge even if individual coefficients have oscillatory subsequences.

Our event is a lattice/local event, precisely the kind of situation in which a local limit or coefficient estimate is required.

---

# 5. Why standard Plancherel de-Poissonization is not automatically enough

Classical Plancherel de-Poissonization successfully transfers many local correlation and edge results from the Bessel-kernel ensemble to fixed \(n\).

But those arguments exploit analytic or monotonic structure specific to the observable being de-Poissonized.

The minimal \(2\)-core event is global and arithmetic.

No ready-made theorem located in this audit verifies the required smoothness or coefficient control for

\[
P_{\mathrm{Pl}_n}(M_n).
\]

So the phrase “de-Poissonize it” is a research programme, not yet a proof.

---

# 6. Direct Plancherel-growth shortcut fails

A tempting approach is to follow the Plancherel growth process on Young diagrams and keep only the \(2\)-core/BG charge.

If this statistic were a Markov lump, one could seek a one-dimensional local CLT.

It is not.

For partitions \(\lambda\) of the same size and with the same charge, the probability that the next added box has residue \(0\) or \(1\) depends on more than the charge.

The audit finds the first failure already at

\[
\boxed{n=4.}
\]

For the \(n=4\), charge-zero group, the next residue-zero probability ranges from

\[
0.2
\]

to

\[
1.
\]

Therefore:

\[
\boxed{
\textbf{the \(2\)-core charge is not a strong Markov lump of Plancherel growth.}
}
\]

This closes the simplest stochastic route.

---

# 7. Exact finite shape of the fixed-\(n\) charge law

The full Plancherel BG-charge distribution was computed exactly through

\[
n=30.
\]

For every audited layer:

\[
\boxed{
\text{the charge distribution is unimodal}
}
\]

and

\[
\boxed{
\text{log-concave on its parity lattice}.
}
\]

So:

\[
\boxed{30/30}
\]

layers pass both properties.

This is exactly the kind of structure one expects from a local-CLT-compatible lattice law.

But finite log-concavity is not enough to prove the asymptotic atom bound.

---

# 8. New finite real-rootedness phenomenon

Define integer weights

\[
W_{n,k}
=
\sum_{\substack{\lambda\vdash n\\
BG(\lambda)=q_{\min}+2k}}
(f^\lambda)^2
\]

and the probability numerator polynomial

\[
\boxed{
G_n(z)
=
\sum_k W_{n,k}z^k.
}
\]

The audit computed \(G_n\) exactly and numerically isolated all its roots through

\[
n=40.
\]

Result:

\[
\boxed{
\textbf{all roots are real and negative for every }1\le n\le40.
}
\]

Thus:

\[
\boxed{40/40}
\]

layers pass the negative-real-root test.

This is substantially stronger than the observed log-concavity.

No all-\(n\) theorem for this specific Plancherel BG polynomial was located in the literature search.

Therefore its current status is:

\[
\boxed{
\textbf{finite-certified structural conjecture.}
}
\]

---

# 9. What real-rootedness would buy us

Suppose the all-\(n\) statement were proved.

Since \(G_n\) has positive coefficients and only negative real zeros,

\[
\frac{G_n(z)}{G_n(1)}
\]

factors as

\[
\prod_{r=1}^{d_n}
\frac{1+\alpha_{n,r}z}{1+\alpha_{n,r}},
\qquad
\alpha_{n,r}>0.
\]

Equivalently, there exist independent Bernoulli variables \(B_{n,r}\) such that

\[
\boxed{
BG_n
\overset d=
q_{\min}
+
2\sum_r B_{n,r}.
}
\]

So the fixed-\(n\) core charge would be an exact shifted **Poisson-binomial** variable.

That would be an enormous simplification.

---

# 10. Conditional local-limit theorem

For sums of independent Bernoulli variables, divergence of the variance gives standard local anti-concentration/local-CLT estimates.

In particular, conditionally on the all-\(n\) real-rootedness theorem,

\[
\max_jP(BG_n=j)
=
O\!\left(
\operatorname{Var}(BG_n)^{-1/2}
\right).
\]

Rostam's Poissonized calculation and our finite fixed-\(n\) data both point to

\[
\operatorname{Var}(BG_n)
\asymp
\sqrt n.
\]

If the fixed-\(n\) variance law

\[
\operatorname{Var}(BG_n)
\sim
\frac{\sqrt n}{\pi}
\]

also holds, then

\[
\boxed{
\max_jP(BG_n=j)
=
O(n^{-1/4}).
}
\]

Since the minimal-core event is one lattice atom,

\[
\boxed{
P_{\mathrm{Pl}_n}(M_n)
=
O(n^{-1/4}),
}
\]

and therefore

\[
\boxed{
Z_n\Rightarrow\delta_0.
}
\]

This is a **conditional theorem**, not the result of 06D.

---

# 11. Why the DPP local CLT is suggestive but not a proof

Forrester and Lebowitz prove a local central limit theorem for the number of points

\[
N(J)
\]

of a determinantal point process in a region \(J\) whenever its variance diverges.

A key ingredient is that the count generating function has zeros on the negative real axis.

That is strikingly parallel to the finite \(G_n\) phenomenon.

Rostam's Poissonized Plancherel argument also uses the determinantal descent-set process.

But:

\[
\boxed{
\textbf{we have not shown that fixed-\(n\) BG charge is itself one DPP point count.}
}
\]

Nor have we transferred a suitable multivariate DPP local CLT through conditioning on total size.

So this is structural guidance, not a bridge theorem.

---

# 12. Fixed-\(n\) numerical evidence

The exact principal-block/minimal-core probabilities are known from the full partition census through

\[
n=40.
\]

Direct fixed-\(n\) Plancherel sampling was then performed by:

1. generating a uniform random permutation in \(S_n\);
2. computing its RSK shape;
3. testing the minimal \(2\)-core/BG-charge condition.

This samples \(\mathrm{Pl}_n\) exactly at the shape level.

For even \(n\):

\[
\begin{array}{c|c}
n & \widehat P(M_n)\\
\hline
100&0.4385\\
200&0.3710\\
500&0.2920\\
1000&0.2633\\
2000&0.19875
\end{array}
\]

A log-log fit gives

\[
\boxed{
\widehat\alpha\approx-0.253
}
\]

for

\[
P(M_n)\approx c n^\alpha,
\]

with

\[
R^2\approx0.984.
\]

Thus the observed exponent is extremely close to

\[
-\frac14.
\]

Odd-layer simulations also decay, with visible finite parity effects.

This remains simulation evidence.

---

# 13. Principal-block interpretation of the same evidence

The same table can be read algebraically:

\[
\frac{\dim B_0(\mathbb C S_n)}{n!}
\]

appears to decay approximately as

\[
n^{-1/4}.
\]

So the conjecture can be phrased equivalently as:

\[
\boxed{
\text{the principal \(2\)-block occupies a vanishing }O(n^{-1/4})
\text{ fraction of the regular representation.}
}
\]

This may be a useful doorway into modular block theory, although no theorem of that form was found in the current search.

---

# 14. What 06D has actually ruled out

Several tempting shortcuts are now unavailable.

We cannot claim:

\[
\text{Poissonized CLT}
\Rightarrow
\text{fixed-\(n\) local CLT}
\]

without de-Poissonization.

We cannot use a one-dimensional charge Markov chain.

We cannot identify our charge distribution with a DPP count.

We cannot promote 40 layers of real-rootedness to an all-\(n\) theorem.

Those negative results matter because each would otherwise create a hidden assumption in the final delta-zero claim.

---

# 15. Main verdict

The requested fixed-\(n\) theorem has **not** been completed.

\[
\boxed{
\textbf{Fixed-\(n\) }P_{\mathrm{Pl}_n}(M_n)\to0:
\ \textbf{OPEN IN THIS AUDIT}.
}
\]

But the remaining gap is now narrow.

The exact target has three equivalent forms:

\[
\boxed{
P_{\mathrm{Pl}_n}(M_n)\to0
}
\]

\[
\boxed{
\frac1{n!}
\sum_{\lambda\in B_0(S_n)}(f^\lambda)^2
\to0
}
\]

or, using BG charge,

\[
\boxed{
P(BG_n=q_{\min})\to0.
}
\]

And 06D produced a new concrete possible proof route:

\[
\boxed{
\textbf{all-\(n\) real-rootedness of the Plancherel BG-charge polynomial.}
}
\]

If that theorem is true, the rest is much closer to standard local-limit probability.

---

# 16. Recommended next probe

The most focused next audit is:

## EHF-PHYS-ALG-06E — BG-Charge Real-Rootedness and Poisson-Binomial Theorem Audit

It should ask:

1. Can
   \[
   G_n(z)
   =
   \sum_k
   \left(
   \sum_{BG=q_{\min}+2k}(f^\lambda)^2
   \right)z^k
   \]
   be identified with a known real-rooted polynomial family?
2. Is there a recurrence
   \[
   G_{n+1}=A_n(z)G_n+B_n(z)G_n'
   \]
   preserving real-rootedness?
3. Can \(G_n\) be expressed as a determinant or characteristic polynomial?
4. Does Schur/RSK theory give a stable-polynomial interpretation?
5. If real-rootedness is proved, derive the Bernoulli parameters and prove
   \[
   \operatorname{Var}(BG_n)\sim\frac{\sqrt n}{\pi}.
   \]
6. Then invoke a Poisson-binomial LCLT to obtain
   \[
   P(M_n)=O(n^{-1/4}).
   \]

This route is now more concrete than another generic attempt to “de-Poissonize” Rostam's theorem.
