# 22. Recommended next audit

The original planned PF-cone audit should now be postponed one more step.

The sharper next target is:

## EHF-PHYS-ALG-06K — Dihedral-Twisted Core PF / Major-Index Transfer Audit

Define separately
\[
C_{n,q}
=
\sum_{\core_2(\lambda)=\kappa(q)}
f^\lambda m_\lambda^{C_n},
\]
and
\[
R_{n,q}
=
\sum_{\core_2(\lambda)=\kappa(q)}
f^\lambda
\operatorname{Tr}
(\Pi|(S^\lambda)^{C_n}),
\]
so that
\[
\boxed{
H_{n,q}
=
\frac12(C_{n,q}+R_{n,q}).
}
\]

Targets:

1. determine whether the cyclic-only polynomial \(C_n(x)\) is PF/real-rooted;
2. determine what the signed reflection correction \(R_n(x)\) does to its zeros;
3. express \(C_{n,q}\) using the Kraśkiewicz–Weyman major-index formula;
4. combine major-index root-of-unity filtering with the \(2\)-core/Bessel-Hankel machinery from 06H/06I;
5. seek a dihedral-twisted Hankel determinant or symmetric-function specialization for \(H_n\);
6. test whether the HC/core PF phenomenon extends beyond \(n=39\) using a recurrence rather than full character enumeration;
7. only then revisit a canonical Fock/vertex-model transfer matrix.

If the cyclic and reflection pieces reveal a stable mechanism, we may finally have the common theorem that the external proposal was gesturing toward—but in a mathematically correct form.


---

# EHF-PHYS-ALG-06K — Dihedral-Twisted Core PF / Major-Index Transfer Audit

**Branch:** EHF-PHYS-ALG  
**Inputs:** PHYS-ALG-06J, the BG/core programme 06D–06I, and standard modular block/major-index theory.  
**Primary target:** determine whether the dihedral reflection correction preserves, damages, or explains the real-rooted HC/core polynomial.

**Final status:** the reflection correction is identically zero **for every \(n\) and every \(2\)-core sector**. Thus the HC/core law is exactly the cyclic-major-index law. All even-order rotation Fourier modes also vanish blockwise, reducing the problem to odd-order rotations. At powers of two the HC core distribution is exactly Plancherel and major index mod \(n\) is independent of the RSK \(2\)-core. The remaining all-\(n\) PF problem is purely cyclic/odd-order.

**Certification:** \(\boxed{336/336}\) expected-outcome checks pass.

---

## 1. Split the dihedral projector

Let
\[
C_n=\langle\tau\rangle,
\qquad
D_n=C_n\sqcup \Pi C_n,
\]
where \(\Pi\) is any reflection.

For a fixed core charge \(q\), define
\[
C_{n,q}
=
\sum_{\operatorname{core}_2(\lambda)=\kappa(q)}
f^\lambda m_\lambda^{C_n},
\]
and
\[
R_{n,q}
=
\sum_{\operatorname{core}_2(\lambda)=\kappa(q)}
f^\lambda
\operatorname{Tr}
\left(
\Pi|(S^\lambda)^{C_n}
\right).
\]

Then
\[
\boxed{
H_{n,q}
=
\frac12(C_{n,q}+R_{n,q}).
}
\]

At the beginning of 06K the natural expectation was that \(R_{n,q}\) might be a signed perturbation of a positive cyclic base.

The computation immediately showed something much stronger:
\[
R_{n,q}=0
\]
on every audited shell.

This is an all-\(n\) theorem, not a numerical coincidence.

---

## 2. The fixed-core block regular character

Define
\[
\boxed{
\Theta_{n,q}(g)
=
\sum_{\substack{\lambda\vdash n\\
\operatorname{core}_2(\lambda)=\kappa(q)}}
f^\lambda\chi^\lambda(g).
}
\]

For \(S_n\), ordinary irreducible characters are in the same \(2\)-block exactly when their indexing partitions have the same \(2\)-core.

Therefore \(\Theta_{n,q}\) is the ordinary character of the \(q\)-block summand of the regular module.

Indeed the ordinary regular character is
\[
\rho_{\rm reg}
=
\sum_{\lambda\vdash n}
f^\lambda\chi^\lambda,
\]
and grouping its irreducible constituents by \(2\)-block gives the \(\Theta_{n,q}\).

---

## 3. Why the block regular character vanishes on reflections

Work in characteristic \(2\).

The group algebra is free, hence projective, over itself.

Each block summand
\[
kS_n e_q
\]
is a direct summand of the regular module and therefore projective.

The ordinary lift character of a projective module vanishes on every \(2\)-singular element.

Thus:
\[
\boxed{
\Theta_{n,q}(g)=0
\qquad
\text{whenever }2\mid\operatorname{ord}(g).
}
\]

Every reflection has order \(2\).

Hence:
\[
\boxed{
\Theta_{n,q}(r)=0
\qquad
\text{for every reflection }r\in D_n.
}
\]

This is the mechanism behind the finite cancellation seen at the start of the audit.

---

## 4. Exact reflection-zero theorem

Since
\[
\operatorname{Tr}
\left(
\Pi|(S^\lambda)^{C_n}
\right)
=
\frac1n
\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}
\chi^\lambda(\Pi\tau^k),
\]
we have
\[
R_{n,q}
=
\frac1n
\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}
\Theta_{n,q}(\Pi\tau^k).
\]

Every element
\[
\Pi\tau^k
\]
is a reflection and therefore has order \(2\).

Consequently every term vanishes:
\[
\boxed{
R_{n,q}=0.
}
\]

Therefore:
\[
\boxed{
H_{n,q}
=
\frac12C_{n,q}
\qquad
\text{for every }n,q.
}
\]

This is the principal theorem of PHYS-ALG-06K.

---

## 5. Reflection does not change the core distribution

The total cyclic-invariant dimension of the regular module is
\[
\sum_q C_{n,q}
=
(n-1)!,
\]
while the HC/dihedral dimension is
\[
\sum_qH_{n,q}
=
\frac{(n-1)!}{2}.
\]

Using
\[
H_{n,q}=C_{n,q}/2,
\]
we get
\[
\boxed{
\frac{H_{n,q}}{(n-1)!/2}
=
\frac{C_{n,q}}{(n-1)!}.
}
\]

So:
\[
\boxed{
\textbf{the normalized dihedral/HC core distribution is exactly the normalized cyclic core distribution.}
}
\]

Reflection changes only the global factor \(1/2\).

It causes no redistribution whatsoever among \(2\)-core sectors.

---

## 6. The cancellation is not shape-by-shape

The projective theorem applies only after summing the complete \(2\)-block regular character.

Individual Specht contributions can be nonzero.

For example at
\[
n=4,\qquad q=0,
\]
the contributions
\[
f^\lambda
\operatorname{Tr}
(\Pi|(S^\lambda)^{C_4})
\]
include
\[
+1,\quad +2,\quad -3
\]
from different partitions.

They sum to
\[
0.
\]

Thus:
\[
\boxed{
\textbf{reflection neutrality is a modular block cancellation, not a per-Specht reflection symmetry.}
}
\]

This distinction is important if we later seek a tableau-level involution.

---

## 7. Cyclic core weights are major-index/RSK counts

Kraśkiewicz–Weyman gives
\[
m_\lambda^{C_n}
=
\#\{
Q\in SYT(\lambda):
\operatorname{maj}(Q)\equiv0\pmod n
\}.
\]

Under RSK,
\[
\pi
\longleftrightarrow
(P(\pi),Q(\pi)),
\]
and
\[
\operatorname{Des}(\pi)
=
\operatorname{Des}(Q(\pi)).
\]

Hence
\[
\operatorname{maj}(\pi)
=
\operatorname{maj}(Q(\pi)).
\]

For each fixed \(Q\) of shape \(\lambda\), there are
\[
f^\lambda
\]
possible insertion tableaux \(P\).

Therefore:
\[
\boxed{
C_{n,q}
=
\#\left\{
\pi\in S_n:
\operatorname{maj}(\pi)\equiv0\pmod n,\ 
BG(\operatorname{shape}_{RSK}\pi)=q
\right\}.
}
\]

This gives a completely combinatorial meaning to the cyclic core polynomial.

---

## 8. Exact HC ↔ conditioned-permutation bridge

Because
\[
H_{n,q}=C_{n,q}/2,
\]
and
\[
|HC_n|=\frac{(n-1)!}{2},
\]
we obtain
\[
\boxed{
\frac{H_{n,q}}{|HC_n|}
=
\frac{C_{n,q}}{(n-1)!}.
}
\]

Major index modulo \(n\) is equidistributed over \(S_n\), so
\[
\#\{\pi:\operatorname{maj}(\pi)\equiv0\pmod n\}
=
(n-1)!.
\]

Hence:
\[
\boxed{
\mathbb P_{\rm HC}
(BG=q)
=
\mathbb P_{\pi\sim{\rm Uniform}(S_n)}
\left(
BG(\operatorname{shape}_{RSK}\pi)=q
\mid
\operatorname{maj}(\pi)\equiv0\pmod n
\right).
}
\]

This is an exact HC/RSK/major-index bridge.

Direct enumeration for
\[
n=4,5,6,7,8
\]
matches the character formula exactly:
\[
\boxed{10/10}
\]
layer-level RSK checks pass.

---

## 9. Only odd-order rotations survive

The cyclic projector gives
\[
C_{n,q}
=
\frac1n
\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}
\Theta_{n,q}(\tau^k).
\]

But projective vanishing says
\[
\Theta_{n,q}(\tau^k)=0
\]
whenever \(\tau^k\) has even order.

Write
\[
n=2^a m,
\qquad m\text{ odd}.
\]

A power of the long cycle has odd order exactly when it lies in the subgroup
\[
\langle\tau^{2^a}\rangle
\cong C_m.
\]

Therefore:
\[
\boxed{
C_{n,q}
=
\frac1n
\sum_{\substack{d\mid n\\d\ {\rm odd}}}
\varphi(d)
\Theta_{n,q}
\bigl(d^{\,n/d}\bigr).
}
\]

Equivalently,
\[
\boxed{
C_{n,q}
=
2^{-a}
\dim
(V_{n,q}^{C_m}),
}
\]
where \(V_{n,q}\) is the regular \(2\)-block component and \(C_m\) is the odd-order rotation subgroup.

Thus the entire cyclic correction to raw Plancherel data lives in the **odd part of \(n\)**.

---

## 10. Exact \(2\)-adic major-index equidistribution

Let
\[
M_{n,q}(r)
\]
be the number of standard tableaux in the block-regular multiplicity space with long-cycle eigenvalue
\[
e^{2\pi ir/n},
\]
equivalently the corresponding permutation count with
\[
\operatorname{maj}\equiv r\pmod n.
\]

Fourier inversion gives
\[
M_{n,q}(r)
=
\frac1n
\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}
e^{-2\pi i rk/n}
\Theta_{n,q}(\tau^k).
\]

Only
\[
k=2^aj
\]
survive.

Therefore the phase in the surviving sum depends only on
\[
r\pmod m.
\]

Hence:
\[
\boxed{
M_{n,q}(r)
\text{ depends only on }r\bmod m.
}
\]

So, within every fixed \(2\)-core block, the \(2\)-primary component of major index modulo \(n\) is exactly uniform.

This is an all-\(n\) theorem.

---

## 11. Power-of-two theorem

If
\[
n=2^a,
\]
then
\[
m=1.
\]

Every nonidentity rotation has even order and vanishes under every block regular character.

Thus:
\[
C_{n,q}
=
\frac1n\Theta_{n,q}(e).
\]

But
\[
\Theta_{n,q}(e)
=
W_{n,q}
=
\sum_{\operatorname{core}_2(\lambda)=\kappa(q)}
(f^\lambda)^2.
\]

Therefore:
\[
\boxed{
C_{n,q}
=
\frac{W_{n,q}}n
}
\]
and
\[
\boxed{
H_{n,q}
=
\frac{W_{n,q}}{2n}.
}
\]

Normalizing:
\[
\boxed{
\frac{H_{n,q}}{|HC_n|}
=
\frac{W_{n,q}}{n!}.
}
\]

The right side is exactly the Plancherel core probability.

Hence:
\[
\boxed{
\textbf{at every power-of-two layer, the HC core distribution is exactly Plancherel.}
}
\]

This is not asymptotic.

It is an exact finite-\(n\) theorem.

---

## 12. Stronger power-of-two independence theorem

At
\[
n=2^a,
\]
all nonidentity Fourier coefficients of the block regular long-cycle character vanish.

Thus every long-cycle eigenvalue occurs equally often inside each \(2\)-core block.

Equivalently:
\[
\boxed{
\#\{
\pi:
BG(\operatorname{shape}_{RSK}\pi)=q,\ 
\operatorname{maj}(\pi)\equiv r\pmod n
\}
=
\frac{W_{n,q}}n
}
\]
for every residue
\[
r\pmod n.
\]

So:
\[
\boxed{
\textbf{major index modulo }n
\textbf{ and RSK }2\textbf{-core are exactly independent when }n\textbf{ is a power of two.}
}
\]

Direct permutation enumeration verifies every residue/core cell at
\[
n=4,8:
\qquad
\boxed{20/20\text{ exact checks}.}
\]

The character proof gives the theorem for all powers of two.

---

## 13. What became of the 06J HC/core PF result?

06J found that the trimmed HC/core polynomial
\[
\widehat H_n(x)
\]
has only negative real roots for
\[
4\le n\le39.
\]

But we now know
\[
\widehat H_n(x)
=
\frac12\widehat C_n(x).
\]

A scalar multiple does not change roots.

Therefore:
\[
\boxed{
\textbf{the 06J PF phenomenon is entirely cyclic-major-index in origin.}
}
\]

Reflection contributes nothing to the polynomial beyond the scalar factor \(1/2\).

The exact Sturm certification remains:
\[
\boxed{
36/36
\text{ cyclic core polynomials real-rooted for }4\le n\le39.
}
\]

---

## 14. Odd-order block twists

For each odd divisor
\[
d\mid n,
\]
define
\[
T_{n,q}^{(d)}
=
\Theta_{n,q}
(d^{\,n/d}),
\]
and the corresponding core polynomial
\[
\mathcal T_{n,d}(x).
\]

Then
\[
\boxed{
\widehat C_n(x)
=
\frac1n
\sum_{\substack{d\mid n\\d\ {\rm odd}}}
\varphi(d)\,
\mathcal T_{n,d}(x),
}
\]
after using the common core-lattice reindexing.

The identity twist
\[
d=1
\]
is the Plancherel/core polynomial:
\[
T_{n,q}^{(1)}=W_{n,q}.
\]

The other twists are signed block-character corrections.

---

## 15. New finite twist signal

Every odd-order twist encountered for
\[
4\le n\le39
\]
was audited.

There are
\[
\boxed{86}
\]
nonzero twist polynomials in the ledger.

Result:
\[
\boxed{
86/86
\text{ are real-rooted}.
}
\]

However they are not all PF polynomials.

Only the identity twists have the familiar positive coefficients/negative roots in this audit.

Nonidentity twists are signed.

Since a nonidentity group element has zero full regular character,
\[
\sum_qT_{n,q}^{(d)}=0,
\]
so
\[
\boxed{x=1}
\]
is always a root of the full nonidentity twist polynomial.

For example:
\[
n=15,\ d=3:
\quad
\text{roots}\approx
0.988811,\ 1,
\]
while
\[
n=36,\ d=3
\]
has roots approximately
\[
-1.63943,\quad
1,\quad
1,\quad
1.00491.
\]

So these Fourier pieces have a different root geometry from the positive cyclic sum.

---

## 16. Why this does not yet prove cyclic PF

The cyclic polynomial is a fixed weighted sum
\[
\sum_{d\mid m}\varphi(d)\mathcal T_{n,d}.
\]

Even if every summand is real-rooted, arbitrary positive linear combinations of real-rooted polynomials need not remain real-rooted.

The twists also have signed coefficients and roots on both sides of zero.

Therefore the finite
\[
86/86
\]
twist result is only a structural clue.

An all-\(n\) theorem would require something stronger, such as:
- a compatible/interlacing family after the correct normalization;
- a common total-positive determinant;
- a root-of-unity specialization of the 06H/06I Hankel object;
- or a direct major-index/core PF theorem.

None is claimed here.

---

## 17. Main verdict

### Reflection contribution
\[
\boxed{\textbf{ZERO EXACTLY, ALL }n}
\]

### Dihedral versus cyclic core law
\[
\boxed{\textbf{IDENTICAL AFTER NORMALIZATION}}
\]

### Major-index/RSK interpretation
\[
\boxed{\textbf{PASS EXACTLY}}
\]

### Odd-order reduction
\[
\boxed{\textbf{PASS EXACTLY}}
\]

### \(2\)-adic major-index equidistribution
\[
\boxed{\textbf{PASS EXACTLY}}
\]

### Power-of-two HC = Plancherel core law
\[
\boxed{\textbf{PASS EXACTLY}}
\]

### Cyclic core PF
\[
\boxed{\textbf{PASS FOR }4\le n\le39,\ \textbf{ALL-}n\textbf{ OPEN}}
\]

### Odd-order twist real-rootedness
\[
\boxed{\textbf{86/86 FINITE TWISTS PASS}}
\]

The problem is now significantly simpler than it was at the start of 06K:

\[
\boxed{
\textbf{there is no dihedral-twist PF problem left.}
}
\]

The only nontrivial selection is the **cyclic/major-index selection**, and only the odd part of \(n\) can alter the Plancherel core law.

## 18. Recommended next audit

### EHF-PHYS-ALG-06L — Odd-Order Rotation Block-Character / Major-Index PF Mechanism Audit

Targets:

1. study
   \[
   \mathcal T_{n,d}(x)
   =
   \sum_q
   \Theta_{n,q}(d^{n/d})x^{j(q)}
   \]
   for odd \(d\);
2. prove or falsify all-\(n\) real-rootedness of these signed twist polynomials;
3. determine whether the family for fixed \(n\) is compatible/interlacing after an appropriate sign or Möbius normalization;
4. combine the root-of-unity major-index formula with the \(2\)-core beta/Hankel determinant from 06H;
5. seek a \(q\)- or root-of-unity deformation of the 06I Toda recurrence;
6. explain why the Euler-totient divisor sum
   \[
   \widehat C_n
   =
   \frac1n
   \sum_{d\mid m}\varphi(d)\mathcal T_{n,d}
   \]
   remains PF;
7. use the exact power-of-two theorem as the \(m=1\) base case.

If this succeeds, it would unify the original Plancherel PF law and the HC/cyclic PF law under one odd-order Fourier/block-character theorem.


---

# EHF-PHYS-ALG-06L — Odd-Order Rotation Block-Character / Major-Index PF Mechanism Audit

**Branch:** EHF-PHYS-ALG  
**Inputs:** PHYS-ALG-06H–06K  
**Primary target:** explain the real-rooted odd-order block-character twist family left after 06K removed reflection and every even-order rotation.

**Final status:** every twist is placed inside one exact root-free mixed Cauchy–Binet determinant and one common 2-periodic content-product 2D-Toda tau function. The \(d=1\) case reduces to the positive Bessel-Hankel Gram determinant already found in 06H; \(d>1\) becomes a residue-decimated mixed/biorthogonal hyper-Bessel pencil. The simplest common-interlacing explanation is exactly false. Real-rootedness extends to all twists through \(n=45\), but the all-\(n\) sign-regular/fixed-size theorem remains open.

**Certification:** \(\boxed{108/108}\) expected-outcome checks pass.

---

## 1. The odd-order twist

Write
\[
n=dm,
\qquad
d\mid n,\qquad d\text{ odd}.
\]

For fixed BG/core charge \(q\), define
\[
\boxed{
\Theta_{n,q}^{(d)}
=
\sum_{\substack{\lambda\vdash n\\
\operatorname{core}_2(\lambda)=\kappa(q)}}
f^\lambda
\chi^\lambda(d^m).
}
\]

The parity-reindexed polynomial in the \(q\)-coordinate is the twist polynomial
\[
\mathcal T_{n,d}(x).
\]

At
\[
d=1,
\]
\[
\chi^\lambda(1^n)=f^\lambda,
\]
so
\[
\boxed{
\Theta_{n,q}^{(1)}=W_{n,q},
}
\]
the original Plancherel/core weight.

Thus the identity twist is exactly the 06D–06I polynomial.

---

## 2. Exact major-index Fourier interpretation

Let
\[
\omega_d=e^{2\pi i/d}.
\]

A power of the long cycle having order \(d\) has cycle type
\[
d^m.
\]

Kraśkiewicz–Weyman identifies the long-cycle eigenvalues on \(S^\lambda\) with tableau major index modulo \(n\). Hence
\[
\boxed{
\chi^\lambda(d^m)
=
\sum_{T\in SYT(\lambda)}
\omega_d^{\operatorname{maj}(T)}.
}
\]

Under RSK,
\[
\operatorname{Des}(\pi)
=
\operatorname{Des}(Q(\pi)),
\]
and therefore
\[
\operatorname{maj}(\pi)
=
\operatorname{maj}(Q(\pi)).
\]

For each recording tableau \(Q\) of shape \(\lambda\), there are
\[
f^\lambda
\]
possible insertion tableaux.

Therefore
\[
\boxed{
\Theta_{n,q}^{(d)}
=
\sum_{\substack{\pi\in S_n\\
BG(\operatorname{shape}_{RSK}\pi)=q}}
\omega_d^{\operatorname{maj}(\pi)}.
}
\]

So each odd-order twist is literally a root-of-unity Fourier coefficient of the joint distribution
\[
(\operatorname{RSK\ 2-core},\operatorname{major\ index}).
\]

For \(d=1\), the Fourier phase is \(1\), recovering the unconditioned Plancherel/RSK core distribution.

---

## 3. Fock/symmetric-function form

Use the Hall inner product with
\[
\langle s_\lambda,s_\mu\rangle=\delta_{\lambda\mu}.
\]

The Frobenius expansions are
\[
p_1^n
=
\sum_{\lambda\vdash n}
f^\lambda s_\lambda,
\]
and
\[
p_d^m
=
\sum_{\lambda\vdash n}
\chi^\lambda(d^m)s_\lambda.
\]

Let \(P_q\) be the Schur-basis projector onto fixed \(2\)-core charge \(q\).

Then
\[
\boxed{
\Theta_{n,q}^{(d)}
=
\langle p_1^n,\;P_qp_d^m\rangle.
}
\]

This is the corrected Fock-space matrix element for the odd-order twist.

It uses exactly the two states that appeared separately in the earlier programme:
- \(p_1^n\): the regular/Plancherel amplitude vector;
- \(p_d^m\): the odd-order rotation character state.

---

## 4. Empty \(d\)-core support

By Murnaghan–Nakayama,
\[
\chi^\lambda(d^m)
\]
is obtained by removing \(m\) rim \(d\)-hooks.

If the \(d\)-core of \(\lambda\) is nonempty, such a sequence cannot reach the empty diagram.

Therefore
\[
\boxed{
\chi^\lambda(d^m)=0
\quad\text{unless}\quad
\operatorname{core}_d(\lambda)=\varnothing.
}
\]

Hence the twist is supported on the intersection
\[
\boxed{
\operatorname{core}_d(\lambda)=\varnothing,
\qquad
\operatorname{core}_2(\lambda)=\kappa(q).
}
\]

For odd \(d\), these are coprime core structures.

This is a more precise statement than viewing the twist as an arbitrary signed correction to the Plancherel core law.

---

# 5. Two Jacobi–Trudi specializations

Fix
\[
r\ge\ell(\lambda)
\]
and define beta numbers
\[
b_i=\lambda_i+r-i.
\]

Write
\[
B=\{b_1>\cdots>b_r\}.
\]

### 5.1 Dimension specialization

Set
\[
p_1=1,
\qquad
p_k=0\quad(k>1).
\]

Then
\[
h_j=\frac1{j!}.
\]

Jacobi–Trudi gives
\[
\boxed{
f^\lambda
=
n!\det A_B,
}
\]
where
\[
\boxed{
A_{b,j}
=
\frac1{(b-r+j)!},
}
\]
with the convention
\[
1/k!=0
\quad(k<0).
\]

### 5.2 \(d\)-cycle specialization

Set
\[
p_d=1,
\qquad
p_k=0\quad(k\ne d).
\]

Then
\[
\sum_{j\ge0}h_jz^j
=
\exp(z^d/d),
\]
so
\[
h_{d\ell}
=
\frac1{d^\ell\ell!},
\qquad
h_j=0\quad(d\nmid j).
\]

Because every nonzero determinant term has total
\[
\sum_i\frac{\lambda_i-i+\sigma(i)}d
=
\frac nd=m,
\]
the common \(d^{-m}\) factor cancels the \(d^m\) in the Frobenius class factor.

Thus
\[
\boxed{
\chi^\lambda(d^m)
=
m!\det C_B^{(d)},
}
\]
where
\[
\boxed{
C_{b,k}^{(d)}
=
\begin{cases}
\dfrac1{((b-r+k)/d)!},
&
b-r+k\ge0,\quad
d\mid(b-r+k),
\\[2mm]
0,&\text{otherwise}.
\end{cases}
}
\]

The audit checked this Jacobi–Trudi formula independently against Murnaghan–Nakayama, shape by shape and core shell by core shell, for every odd divisor through
\[
n=12.
\]

All comparisons pass exactly.

---

# 6. The mixed Cauchy–Binet determinant

Define the diagonal beta-site weight
\[
Y_b(t,u)
=
t^b
u^{\mathbf1_{b\ {\rm even}}}.
\]

Let
\[
Y=\operatorname{diag}(Y_0,Y_1,\ldots).
\]

Define the finite \(r\times r\) matrix
\[
\boxed{
K_{r,d}(t,u)
=
A^\top Y C^{(d)}.
}
\]

Cauchy–Binet gives
\[
\boxed{
\det K_{r,d}(t,u)
=
\sum_{\substack{B\subset\mathbb Z_{\ge0}\\|B|=r}}
\det A_B
\det C_B^{(d)}
t^{\sum_{b\in B}b}
u^{N_{\rm even}(B)}.
}
\]

This is the odd-order analogue of the 06H beta-set determinant.

The important distinction is that it is a **mixed determinant**:
\[
A_B\ne C_B^{(d)}
\]
for
\[
d>1.
\]

---

# 7. Exact fixed-\(n\) twist coefficient theorem

Take \(r\) even and large enough that every partition of \(n\) is represented; for example
\[
r\ge n.
\]

As in 06H,
\[
\sum_{b\in B}b
=
n+\frac{r(r-1)}2,
\]
and
\[
N_{\rm even}(B)
=
\frac r2+BG(\lambda).
\]

Therefore
\[
\boxed{
\Theta_{n,q}^{(d)}
=
n!m!\,
\left[
t^{\,n+r(r-1)/2}
u^{\,r/2+q}
\right]
\det K_{r,d}(t,u).
}
\]

This is an exact all-\(n\) root-free determinant/coefficient formula for every odd-order twist.

No character-table enumeration or previously known roots enter its construction.

This is the main algebraic theorem of PHYS-ALG-06L.

---

# 8. Odd \(d\) gives a residue-decimated hyper-Bessel pencil

The congruence condition in \(C^{(d)}\) sets
\[
b=r-k+d\ell.
\]

Then
\[
b-r+j
=
d\ell+j-k.
\]

For even \(r\) and odd \(d\),
\[
b\text{ is even}
\iff
\ell\equiv k\pmod2.
\]

Hence
\[
\boxed{
K_{jk}^{(d)}(t,u)
=
t^{\,r-k}
\sum_{\ell\ge0}
\frac{
(t^d)^\ell
u^{\mathbf1_{\ell\equiv k\pmod2}}
}{
(d\ell+j-k)!\,\ell!
},
}
\]
with terms having negative factorial arguments omitted.

By the multiplication formula for the Gamma function, these are parity dissections of generalized hypergeometric
\[
{}_0F_d
\]
type series—hyper-Bessel-type functions.

So the odd-order rotation problem has a concrete analytic matrix pencil.

---

# 9. Why \(d=1\) is special

For
\[
d=1,
\]
\[
C^{(1)}=A.
\]

Therefore
\[
\boxed{
K_{r,1}
=
A^\top Y A.
}
\]

For
\[
t>0,\quad u\ge0,
\]
this is a Gram/positive-semidefinite construction.

It is simply a different basis representation of the Bessel-Hankel determinant from 06H.

Thus:
\[
\boxed{
\text{06H/06I Plancherel}
=
d=1\text{ member of the 06L mixed determinant family}.
}
\]

For
\[
d>1,
\]
the second matrix is residue-decimated:
\[
C^{(d)}\ne A.
\]

The determinant is biorthogonal rather than Gram.

The PSD proof disappears.

This is exactly the mathematical place where the identity twist and nonidentity twists diverge.

---

# 10. Common 2D-Toda master object

There is an even more compact unification.

Define
\[
\boxed{
\Tau(u;\mathbf t,\mathbf s)
=
\sum_\lambda
u^{BG(\lambda)}
s_\lambda(\mathbf t)
s_\lambda(\mathbf s).
}
\]

Since
\[
BG(\lambda)
=
\sum_{\square\in\lambda}
(-1)^{c(\square)},
\]
we have
\[
\boxed{
u^{BG(\lambda)}
=
\prod_{\square\in\lambda}
u^{(-1)^{c(\square)}}.
}
\]

This is a content-product coefficient with period \(2\) in the content.

Therefore \(\Tau\) belongs to the standard Orlov–Shcherbin/Harnad class of diagonal content-product hypergeometric \(2\)-Toda tau-functions.

Now specialize the first alphabet by
\[
p_1=a,
\qquad p_k=0\ (k>1),
\]
so
\[
s_\lambda=\frac{f^\lambda}{n!}a^n.
\]

Specialize the second by
\[
p_d=b,
\qquad p_k=0\ (k\ne d).
\]

For
\[
n=dm,
\]
\[
s_\lambda
=
\frac{\chi^\lambda(d^m)}{d^mm!}b^m.
\]

Hence
\[
\boxed{
\sum_{\lambda\vdash n}
u^{BG(\lambda)}
f^\lambda\chi^\lambda(d^m)
=
n!d^mm!\,
[a^nb^m]\Tau(u;a,b).
}
\]

So:
\[
\boxed{
\textbf{the Plancherel BG law and every odd-order rotation twist are coefficients of the same 2-periodic content-product Toda tau-function.}
}
\]

This is the most convincing common representation-theoretic/integrable structure found so far.

It does **not** by itself imply real-rootedness.

---

# 11. The forced \(x=1\) root

For
\[
d>1,
\]
the class
\[
d^m
\]
is nonidentity.

Summing over all core blocks:
\[
\sum_q\Theta_{n,q}^{(d)}
=
\sum_{\lambda\vdash n}
f^\lambda\chi^\lambda(d^m).
\]

The right-hand side is the regular character evaluated at a nonidentity element.

Therefore
\[
\boxed{
\sum_q\Theta_{n,q}^{(d)}=0.
}
\]

After parity reindexing,
\[
\boxed{
\mathcal T_{n,d}(1)=0.
}
\]

So every nonidentity twist contains
\[
\boxed{x-1}
\]
as an exact factor.

The same statement follows from the major-index form:
\[
\sum_{\pi\in S_n}\omega_d^{\operatorname{maj}(\pi)}=0.
\]

This explains the ubiquitous root \(1\) seen in 06K.

---

# 12. The common-interlacing idea fails

One plausible explanation for the 86/86 result was that, for fixed \(n\), the residual twist polynomials after removing \(x-1\) might form a common Sturm/interlacing family as \(d\) varies.

This is false.

At
\[
n=36,
\]
remove the forced \(x-1\) factor.

For
\[
d=3,
\]
the primitive residual cubic is
\[
1193967105210x^3
-
436372552581x^2
-
2724630128906x
+
1967035576275.
\]

Its roots lie in exact rational isolating intervals around
\[
-1.63943,\qquad
1^{-},\qquad
1.00491.
\]

For
\[
d=9,
\]
the primitive residual cubic is
\[
14726469555x^3
-
34648596786x^2
+
25031310545x
-
5109183315,
\]
with roots around
\[
0.343861,\qquad
1^{-},\qquad
1.00895.
\]

Their ordered roots do not alternate.

Therefore:
\[
\boxed{
\textbf{naive same-\(n\) common interlacing across odd divisors is rejected exactly.}
}
\]

The cyclic Euler-totient sum cannot be justified by a simple common-interlacing theorem of this form.

---

# 13. Fresh out-of-sample twist census

06K had
\[
86
\]
odd-order twist polynomials through
\[
n=39,
\]
all real-rooted.

PHYS-ALG-06L generated every **nonidentity** odd-divisor twist for
\[
40\le n\le45
\]
directly from Murnaghan–Nakayama characters.

There are
\[
12
\]
new nonidentity twists.

Exact Sturm counting gives
\[
\boxed{
12/12\text{ real-rooted}.
}
\]

The six identity twists
\[
d=1,\qquad n=40,\ldots,45
\]
are already covered by the exact 06I Plancherel root theorem.

Therefore the combined finite statement is now:
\[
\boxed{
104/104
\text{ odd-divisor twist polynomials are real-rooted for }
4\le n\le45.
}
\]

This remains finite certification.

---

# 14. Grand mixed-pencils: a new spectral clue

For fixed
\[
r,d,t>0,
\]
write
\[
K_{r,d}(t,u)
=
K_{\rm odd}(t)+uK_{\rm even}(t).
\]

For \(d=1\), this is a PSD/Hermitian pencil and its root theorem is exact.

For \(d>1\), neither matrix need form the same Gram pair.

Nevertheless PHYS-ALG-06L tested
\[
r=2,4,6,
\]
\[
d=1,3,5,7,
\]
and
\[
t=0.25,0.5,1,2.
\]

That is
\[
48
\]
grand mixed pencils.

Every finite generalized eigenvalue was numerically real:
\[
\boxed{48/48}.
\]

For \(d>1\), roots occur on both signs, in agreement with the signed twist geometry.

This is **diagnostic evidence only**.

But it points toward a possible theory of:
- sign-regular matrix pencils;
- oscillatory/generalized oscillatory matrices;
- or total-positive generalized-hypergeometric kernels.

The literature contains strong total-positivity and Pólya-frequency results for generalized hypergeometric kernels, but no theorem located in this audit applies directly to the present residue-decimated mixed pencil.

---

# 15. What 06L has ruled out

The following candidate explanations are now closed or insufficient:

### Pure PSD determinantal structure
Works only for
\[
d=1.
\]

### Common interlacing of odd-divisor twists
\[
\boxed{\text{false}.}
\]

### “Each twist is real-rooted, therefore the cyclic sum is”
Not valid: linear combinations of real-rooted signed polynomials need not retain real roots.

### Generic 2D-Toda integrability
The common tau-function is exact, but Toda membership alone does not imply the fixed-size real-root property.

---

# 16. What remains genuinely plausible

The evidence now points to a more specific mechanism.

The entry kernel
\[
\frac1{(d\ell+j-k)!\ell!}
\]
belongs to the generalized-hypergeometric/factorial family.

At \(d=1\), this gives the Bessel/PF kernel.

At odd \(d>1\), the matrix is a parity-dissection of a generalized hyper-Bessel kernel plus a residue-decimation in the second Jacobi–Trudi factor.

The persistent real generalized spectrum of the grand pencil suggests:
\[
\boxed{
\textbf{a sign-regular or oscillatory matrix-pair theorem may be the missing grand-canonical mechanism.}
}
\]

But even that would leave the same type of question encountered in 06H:
\[
\boxed{
\textbf{why does fixed-total beta-sum coefficient extraction retain real-rootedness?}
}
\]

So there are now two precise theorem gaps rather than one vague one.

---

# 17. Main verdict

### Major-index Fourier mechanism
\[
\boxed{\textbf{PASS EXACTLY}}
\]

### Empty odd-\(d\) core reduction
\[
\boxed{\textbf{PASS EXACTLY}}
\]

### Mixed Jacobi–Trudi / Cauchy–Binet determinant
\[
\boxed{\textbf{PASS EXACTLY}}
\]

### Common content-product 2D-Toda origin
\[
\boxed{\textbf{PASS EXACTLY}}
\]

### Recovery of 06H at \(d=1\)
\[
\boxed{\textbf{PASS EXACTLY}}
\]

### Common interlacing
\[
\boxed{\textbf{REJECT}}
\]

### Twist real-root census
\[
\boxed{\textbf{104/104 PASS through }n=45}
\]

### Grand mixed-pencil real spectrum
\[
\boxed{\textbf{48/48 FINITE DIAGNOSTIC PASS}}
\]

### All-\(n\) twist real-root theorem
\[
\boxed{\textbf{OPEN}}
\]

PHYS-ALG-06L has therefore converted the odd-rotation mystery into a concrete matrix problem:

> **Why is the residue-decimated mixed hyper-Bessel pencil sign-regular/real-spectral, and why does its factorial fixed-total coefficient slice retain real-rootedness?**

That is a substantially narrower and more algebraic target than the original major-index question.

---

# 18. Recommended next audit

## EHF-PHYS-ALG-06M — Mixed Hyper-Bessel Pencil Sign-Regularity / Microcanonical Root Theorem Audit

Targets:

1. prove or falsify real generalized spectrum of
   \[
   K_{\rm odd}(t)+uK_{\rm even}(t)
   \]
   for every odd \(d\), \(r\), and \(t>0\);

2. factor the Jacobi–Trudi matrices into Pascal/total-positive and residue-permutation pieces;

3. determine the exact sign-regular signature of the residue-decimated matrix \(C^{(d)}\);

4. use generalized hypergeometric total-positivity results to prove, or delimit, the grand-pencil theorem;

5. derive a planar-network or compound-matrix representation if possible;

6. then attack the microcanonical coefficient
   \[
   [t^{\,n+r(r-1)/2}]
   \det K_{r,d}(t,u)
   \]
   directly;

7. compare \(d=1\) Bessel-PF and \(d>1\) sign-regular cases under one theorem;

8. use the common content-product Toda tau only after the matrix sign structure is understood.

A successful 06M would explain the 104/104 twist phenomenon at its actual algebraic source and would give the best current route toward the all-\(n\) cyclic/HC PF theorem.


---

# EHF-PHYS-ALG-06M — Mixed Hyper-Bessel Pencil Sign-Regularity / Microcanonical Root Theorem Audit

**Branch:** EHF-PHYS-ALG  
**Inputs:** PHYS-ALG-06L and the BG/core determinant programme 06H–06K  
**Primary target:** prove or falsify the proposed sign-regular/oscillatory grand-pencil mechanism, then determine whether fixed-total coefficient extraction inherits real-rootedness.

**Final status:** both generic mechanisms are false. The mixed grand pencil has a rigorously certified complex-root example, and a fixed-total coefficient of the same determinant can have complex roots before bead stabilization. The exact \(104/104\) full symmetric-group twist census through \(n=45\) survives untouched. The missing structure therefore belongs to the **stabilized full Schur/representation coefficient**, not to generic matrix-pencil stability.

**Certification:** all recorded expected-outcome and scope checks pass.

---

## 1. Starting point

PHYS-ALG-06L produced, for odd \(d\),

\[
K_{r,d}(t,u)
=
A^\top
\operatorname{diag}\!\left(t^b u^{\mathbf 1_{b\ {\rm even}}}\right)
C^{(d)},
\]

with

\[
A_{b,j}
=
\frac1{(b-r+j)!},
\]

and

\[
C^{(d)}_{b,k}
=
\begin{cases}
\dfrac1{((b-r+k)/d)!},
&
d\mid(b-r+k),\ b-r+k\ge0,
\\
0,&\text{otherwise}.
\end{cases}
\]

For \(d=1\),

\[
C^{(1)}=A,
\]

so the determinant is Gram/PSD and reduces to the Bessel-Hankel construction of 06H.

For \(d>1\), 06L found \(48/48\) sampled positive-\(t\) pencils with real generalized spectrum and proposed sign-regularity as a possible explanation.

06M tests that proposal directly.

---

# 2. The left kernel really is totally nonnegative

The sequence

\[
a_k=\frac1{k!},\qquad k\ge0,
\]

has generating function

\[
\sum_{k\ge0}\frac{z^k}{k!}=e^z.
\]

It is a classical PF\(_\infty\) sequence.

Hence its semi-infinite Toeplitz matrix is totally nonnegative.

The Jacobi–Trudi indexing orders the columns oppositely to the standard Toeplitz convention. After the fixed reversal \(j\mapsto r+1-j\), the matrix is a submatrix of the factorial Toeplitz matrix. Therefore:

\[
\boxed{
A\text{ is TNN up to this fixed column reversal.}
}
\]

Finite exact minor censuses were included as a certification layer.

---

# 3. The residue-decimated right kernel is TNN only after splitting into residue blocks

The condition

\[
d\mid b-r+k
\]

means that column \(k\) only interacts with rows

\[
b\equiv r-k\pmod d.
\]

If rows are grouped by \(b\bmod d\) and columns by \(r-k\bmod d\), then

\[
C^{(d)}
\]

becomes block diagonal.

Inside each nonzero block the entries reduce, after integer shifts, to

\[
\frac1{(\ell-j)!}
\]

or an equivalent factorial Toeplitz form.

Thus, after the corresponding fixed reversal of columns inside each residue block, every individual residue block is TNN.

So the constituent matrices really do contain total positivity.

The difficulty is the **interleaving of the residue blocks with the even/odd beta split**.

---

# 4. The parity mixed matrices are not sign-regular

Write

\[
K_{\rm even}(t)
=
A^\top D_{\rm even}(t)C^{(d)},
\]

\[
K_{\rm odd}(t)
=
A^\top D_{\rm odd}(t)C^{(d)}.
\]

If an ordinary Gantmacher-Krein/sign-regular explanation were present, all minors of each fixed order would have a prescribed common sign, possibly after fixed diagonal row/column signs.

That fails already at

\[
r=4,\qquad d=3.
\]

For \(K_{\rm even}\), order-two minors include leading small-\(t\) terms

\[
+\frac16t^8
\]

and

\[
-t^6.
\]

For \(K_{\rm odd}\), order-two minors include

\[
+\frac1{2520}t^{12}
\]

and

\[
-\frac13t^8.
\]

Thus each parity matrix has order-two minors of both signs for sufficiently small positive \(t\).

Even more strongly, the \(2\times2\) **matrix of minor signs** contains a rectangle whose four signs multiply to \(-1\).

For the even parity example one such rectangle has signs

\[
(+,-,+,+).
\]

For the odd parity example:

\[
(-,-,-,+).
\]

If minor signs could be repaired by fixed diagonal row/column signs, every such rectangle product would equal \(+1\).

Therefore:

\[
\boxed{
\textbf{no fixed diagonal sign scaling turns these mixed matrices into an ordinary sign-regular family.}
}
\]

So the simplest SR/oscillatory-matrix theorem is unavailable.

---

# 5. The grand real-spectrum conjecture is false

The stronger surprise is that the real generalized spectrum itself does not persist for all \(t>0\).

Take

\[
\boxed{
r=3,\qquad d=3,\qquad t=3.
}
\]

Consider

\[
P(u)
=
\det\left(
K_{\rm odd}(3)+uK_{\rm even}(3)
\right).
\]

Each matrix entry is a convergent positive factorial series.

06M summed the series to a fixed depth and bounded the remaining tail by a geometric bound obtained from the exact ratio of two consecutive same-parity terms.

This gives rigorous intervals for the cubic coefficients.

Propagating those intervals through the cubic discriminant gives

\[
\boxed{
\Delta(P)
\in
[-3.5868184532661\times10^{10},
 -3.5868184532578\times10^{10}]
}
\]

(up to the printed interval rounding).

The entire interval is negative.

Hence \(P\) has exactly one real root and a nonreal conjugate pair.

High-precision root values are

\[
\boxed{
-1.0487524370396
}
\]

and

\[
\boxed{
1.1361454946529
\pm
0.1762426800988\,i.
}
\]

Therefore:

\[
\boxed{
\textbf{the grand mixed hyper-Bessel pencil is not real-spectral for all positive }t.
}
\]

This formally closes the principal sign-regularity target proposed at the end of 06L.

---

# 6. What happened to the 48/48 result from 06L?

Nothing was wrong with those calculations.

They sampled

\[
r=2,4,6,
\]

\[
d=1,3,5,7,
\]

and

\[
t=0.25,0.5,1,2.
\]

All sampled pencils were real-spectral.

06M broadened the parameter search and found complex-root windows.

For example at

\[
r=3,d=3,
\]

the roots are real at \(t=2\) and \(t=2.5\), become a complex pair near \(t\approx2.64\), remain complex at \(t=3\), and become real again by \(t=4\).

So the grand pencil has a genuine **real-to-complex-to-real spectral transition**.

The 06L statement is therefore reclassified as:

\[
\boxed{
\textbf{finite real-spectrum regions exist, but there is no global grand-pencil theorem.}
}
\]

---

# 7. Generic fixed-total coefficient extraction fails too

One might still hope that the fixed-total coefficient

\[
[t^M]\det K_{r,d}(t,u)
\]

is always real-rooted even if the full grand determinant is not.

That is also false.

Take

\[
r=4,\qquad d=5,
\]

and the total beta sum corresponding formally to

\[
n=20.
\]

The parity-reindexed coefficient polynomial is, after clearing denominators and taking the primitive integer form,

\[
\boxed{
92112000u^2
-
193538251u
+
104770560.
}
\]

Its exact discriminant is

\[
\boxed{
-1145448690740999<0.
}
\]

Thus its roots are

\[
\boxed{
1.0505593788
\pm
0.1837137289\,i.
}
\]

Therefore:

\[
\boxed{
\textbf{generic microcanonical extraction from the mixed determinant does not preserve real-rootedness.}
}
\]

---

# 8. Why that counterexample does not contradict the twist census

For the exact symmetric-group twist formula, the beta-bead number must be large enough to represent **all** partitions in the layer.

A sufficient stable choice is

\[
r\ge n.
\]

The counterexample used

\[
r=4<n=20.
\]

It therefore computes a truncated length-restricted coefficient of the same determinant architecture, not the full \(S_{20}\) character sum.

Now compare with the actual full twist.

For

\[
n=20,\qquad d=5,
\]

the full core-twist polynomial from PHYS-ALG-06K is

\[
-4223664128
+
8449425408u
-
4225761280u^2.
\]

Its primitive factorization is

\[
\boxed{
-(u-1)(2015u-2014).
}
\]

Hence the full roots are

\[
\boxed{
u=1,
\qquad
u=\frac{2014}{2015}\approx0.9995037221.
}
\]

So:

\[
\boxed{
\textbf{the full stabilized representation coefficient is real-rooted even when an insufficient-bead coefficient of the same determinant is not.}
}
\]

This is one of the most informative outcomes of 06M.

---

# 9. Stabilization is not a cosmetic technicality

The determinant

\[
\det K_{r,d}
\]

contains a hierarchy of length-truncated partition sums as \(r\) varies.

Before stabilization, the fixed-total coefficient need not have the observed root property.

After all partition lengths are included, the coefficient becomes the genuine symmetric-group matrix element

\[
\langle p_1^n,P_qp_d^{n/d}\rangle.
\]

The empirical theorem is therefore more accurately phrased as:

> **The stabilized full Schur coefficient is real-rooted, not the generic determinant coefficient.**

That eliminates a large class of purely analytic proofs that ignore the stable representation-theoretic limit.

---

# 10. The 104/104 twist result is untouched

PHYS-ALG-06L certified every odd-divisor full twist through

\[
n=45.
\]

The count is

\[
\boxed{
104/104.
}
\]

06M has not found a counterexample to those polynomials.

Instead it has shown that two proposed explanations for them are false:

1. grand-canonical real spectrum;
2. generic fixed-total coefficient stability.

Thus the finite phenomenon becomes **more representation-specific**, not weaker.

---

# 11. What total positivity still contributes

The total-positive pieces are not useless.

We now know:

\[
A
\quad\text{is TNN up to a fixed column reversal},
\]

and residue-split blocks of

\[
C^{(d)}
\quad\text{are TNN after fixed within-block column reversals}.
\]

This explains why so much real-spectrum behavior appears in finite parameter regions.

Generalized hypergeometric kernels have substantial total-positivity theory, including strict TP results for appropriate parameter configurations. citeturn954578search1turn954578search10

But those theorems do not survive the exact residue interleaving and parity compression in the form required here.

The explicit 06M counterexamples prove that no theorem which predicts universal TP/sign-regularity of the present mixed pencil can be correct.

---

# 12. Multiple orthogonality now looks more relevant than ordinary TP

The structure

\[
A^\top D C^{(d)}
\]

is naturally **biorthogonal**:

- one family is the usual factorial/Charlier basis;
- the second family is residue-decimated modulo \(d\);
- parity splits the support into two interlaced sublattices.

This resembles multiple Charlier and discrete multiple-orthogonal systems, including systems defined on scaled lattices and star-like residue structures. Multiple Charlier systems are known to have real-zero and interlacing phenomena under appropriate normality assumptions. citeturn696639search5turn668605search6turn668605search7

Recent work gives particularly useful criteria: zeros and interlacing of multiple orthogonal polynomials can be characterized in terms of **normality of Christoffel-transformed systems**. citeturn696639search0turn668605search2

That suggests a sharper theorem hunt.

Instead of proving that the mixed grand pencil is sign-regular—which is false—we should ask:

\[
\boxed{
\textbf{Is the stabilized full twist polynomial a type-I, type-II, or mixed multiple-orthogonal polynomial for a normal factorial/residue system?}
}
\]

If yes, its real roots might follow from normality even though the unstabilized determinant and the grand pencil can have complex roots.

---

# 13. Why the stable Schur coefficient can be stronger than the grand object

This is not unprecedented conceptually.

A grand generating function packages many coefficient sectors at once.

Properties of that full function need not coincide with properties of a special representation-theoretic coefficient.

Here the stable coefficient additionally imposes:

- exact partition size \(n\);
- all partition lengths;
- the Schur/Jacobi-Trudi determinant structure;
- empty \(d\)-core support;
- fixed \(2\)-core charge;
- and the full symmetric-group character cancellation.

Those conditions are absent from a generic finite-rank microcanonical coefficient.

Thus the root theorem may arise only after the entire representation category has stabilized at grade \(n\).

---

# 14. Main verdict

## Constituent factorial total positivity
\[
\boxed{\textbf{PASS}}
\]

## Residue-block total positivity of \(C^{(d)}\)
\[
\boxed{\textbf{PASS}}
\]

## Mixed parity matrix sign-regularity
\[
\boxed{\textbf{FAIL}}
\]

## Diagonal-sign rescue
\[
\boxed{\textbf{FAIL}}
\]

## Universal grand mixed-pencil real spectrum
\[
\boxed{\textbf{FAIL}}
\]

with a certified counterexample at

\[
\boxed{r=3,\ d=3,\ t=3.}
\]

## Generic fixed-total coefficient real-rootedness
\[
\boxed{\textbf{FAIL}}
\]

with the exact polynomial

\[
\boxed{
92112000x^2-193538251x+104770560.
}
\]

## Full stable twist
\[
\boxed{\textbf{still finite-PASS through }n=45.}
\]

So the problem has moved again.

The missing theorem is **not**:

> Why is the mixed hyper-Bessel pencil sign-regular?

It is:

> **Why does the stabilized full Schur/character coefficient become real-rooted even though both its grand parent and its finite-rank truncations can fail?**

That is a much more representation-theoretic question.

---

# 15. Recommended next audit

## EHF-PHYS-ALG-06N — Stable Schur Coefficient / Multiple-Orthogonality Normality Audit

Targets:

1. identify the full twist
   \[
   \mathcal T_{n,d}(x)
   \]
   as a type-I, type-II, or mixed multiple-orthogonal polynomial if possible;

2. derive its orthogonality relations directly from
   \[
   \langle p_1^n,P_qp_d^{n/d}\rangle;
   \]

3. determine whether the residue-decimated factorial weights form an AT, Nikishin, Angelesco, or other perfect system after the **stable** Schur projection;

4. apply the 2026 normality/Christoffel-transform zero criteria if the identification succeeds;

5. compare finite bead rank \(r<n\) against the stable \(r\ge n\) system to understand exactly what normality is gained at stabilization;

6. exploit simultaneous empty \(d\)-core and fixed \(2\)-core structure, possibly through the \(2d\)-abacus;

7. search for a direct stable Jacobi-Trudi determinant with a planar-network or nonintersecting-path model whose path endpoints encode both cores;

8. only if MOP normality fails, return to the PF-cone/Toda route.

PHYS-ALG-06M therefore closes the proposed sign-regular grand route cleanly and gives us a better-defined target: **stable representation-theoretic normality**.


---

# EHF-PHYS-ALG-06N — Stable Schur Coefficient / Multiple-Orthogonality Normality Audit

**Branch:** EHF-PHYS-ALG  
**Inputs:** PHYS-ALG-06L/06M and the earlier BG/core programme.  
**Primary target:** test whether the full bead-stabilized twist is genuinely a multiple-orthogonal polynomial whose real zeros could follow from normality.

**Final status:** the standard/natural multiple-Charlier/MOP identification is rejected **before** the normality step. In its place, 06N obtains an exact all-\(n\) signed \(d\)-quotient convolution formula. For odd \(d\), both the BG statistic and the \(d^m\)-character factor through the \(d\)-quotient; the entire nontrivial dependence is concentrated in the signed full-dimension coupling \(\varepsilon_d(\lambda)f^\lambda\).

**Certification:** \(\boxed{7106/7106}\) expected-outcome, finite-exact, and scope checks pass.

---

## 1. What would have to be true for the MOP route

For a fixed system of measures
\[
\boldsymbol\mu=(\mu_1,\ldots,\mu_r)
\]
and a fixed multi-index
\[
\mathbf n=(n_1,\ldots,n_r),
\]
a standard type-II multiple orthogonal polynomial
\[
P_{\mathbf n}(x)
\]
is one monic polynomial of exact degree
\[
|\mathbf n|=n_1+\cdots+n_r.
\]

Type-I multiple orthogonality instead produces a vector of component polynomials.

The normality and Christoffel-transform zero theorems apply **after** such a measure system and multi-index have been identified.

So 06N first asks whether the stable twist actually has this structure.

---

# 2. Littlewood eliminates almost all partitions

Let
\[
n=dm,
\qquad d\text{ odd}.
\]

For the twist
\[
\Theta_{n,q}^{(d)}
=
\sum_{\core_2(\lambda)=\kappa(q)}
f^\lambda\chi^\lambda(d^m),
\]
Littlewood's multiple formula gives
\[
\boxed{
\chi^\lambda(d^m)=0
\quad\text{unless}\quad
\core_d(\lambda)=\varnothing.
}
\]

If the \(d\)-core is empty, write the \(d\)-quotient as
\[
\boldsymbol\nu
=
(\nu_0,\ldots,\nu_{d-1}),
\qquad
m_j=|\nu_j|,
\qquad
\sum_jm_j=m.
\]

Then
\[
\boxed{
\chi^\lambda(d^m)
=
\varepsilon_d(\lambda)
\frac{m!}{m_0!\cdots m_{d-1}!}
\prod_{j=0}^{d-1}f^{\nu_j}.
}
\]

Thus the entire character magnitude factors through the quotient.

---

# 3. BG rank also factorizes over the odd-\(d\) quotient

Berkovich and Garvan proved an identity connecting BG rank with the Littlewood \(d\)-decomposition.

For odd \(d\),
\[
BG(\lambda)
=
BG(\core_d\lambda)
+
\sum_{j=0}^{d-1}
(-1)^{j+n_j}
BG(\nu_j),
\]
where the \(n_j\) encode the \(d\)-core.

On the twist support,
\[
\core_d(\lambda)=\varnothing.
\]

For the empty core,
\[
n_j=0
\]
for every runner.

Therefore:
\[
\boxed{
BG(\lambda)
=
\sum_{j=0}^{d-1}
(-1)^jBG(\nu_j).
}
\]

The core-charge fugacity becomes a product over quotient components:
\[
u^{BG(\lambda)}
=
\prod_{j=0}^{d-1}
u^{(-1)^jBG(\nu_j)}.
\]

So both pieces that looked mysterious in the original partition sum—the \(d\)-cycle character and the BG exponent—have now separated across the \(d\)-quotient.

---

# 4. Exact stable quotient-convolution theorem

Let
\[
\Lambda_d(\nu_0,\ldots,\nu_{d-1})
\]
denote the inverse Littlewood map with empty \(d\)-core.

Combining the two preceding theorems gives
\[
\boxed{
\begin{aligned}
\mathcal T_{dm,d}(u)
={}&
m!
\sum_{\substack{\nu_0,\ldots,\nu_{d-1}\\
\sum_j|\nu_j|=m}}
\varepsilon_d(\Lambda_d(\boldsymbol\nu))
f^{\Lambda_d(\boldsymbol\nu)}
\\
&\quad\times
\prod_{j=0}^{d-1}
\frac{f^{\nu_j}}{|\nu_j|!}
\;
u^{\sum_j(-1)^jBG(\nu_j)}.
\end{aligned}
}
\]

This is an exact all-\(n\) formula for every odd-order twist.

It contains no root information.

It also gives a sharper description of the remaining problem:

\[
\boxed{
\textbf{the statistic factorizes; the amplitude does not.}
}
\]

---

# 5. The remaining amplitude is a genuine cross-runner coupling

Take a beta representation with
\[
r=ds
\]
beads and empty \(d\)-core.

Each runner has \(s\) beads. Write
\[
B
=
\bigsqcup_{a=0}^{d-1}
\{a+dx:x\in X_a\}.
\]

Then the Vandermonde splits, up to the fixed orientation sign, as
\[
\boxed{
|\Delta(B)|
=
d^{\,d\binom{s}{2}}
\prod_{a=0}^{d-1}|\Delta(X_a)|
\prod_{0\le a<b<d}
\prod_{\substack{x\in X_a\\y\in X_b}}
|d(x-y)+a-b|.
}
\]

Since
\[
f^\lambda
=
n!\frac{|\Delta(B)|}{\prod_{b\in B}b!},
\]
the cross-runner product remains in the full dimension.

Therefore:
\[
\boxed{
f^\lambda
\textbf{ is not a product of independent quotient-component weights.}
}
\]

This is the odd-\(d\) analogue of the cross-runner obstruction found for the \(2\)-quotient in 06H.

---

# 6. Exact finite validation

PHYS-ALG-06N independently reconstructed the \(d\)-core and \(d\)-quotient and tested the two classical formulas against direct Murnaghan–Nakayama character computation.

For all audited odd divisors through
\[
n=21,
\]
there are
\[
\boxed{1464}
\]
empty-\(d\)-core shape records.

Every one satisfies
\[
BG(\lambda)
=
\sum_j(-1)^jBG(\nu_j)
\]
and
\[
|\chi^\lambda(d^m)|
=
\frac{m!}{\prod_jm_j!}\prod_jf^{\nu_j}.
\]

The shellwise quotient reconstruction also reproduces every direct twist coefficient exactly.

---

# 7. Why this is not a standard multiple-Charlier polynomial

The \(d\)-quotient seems at first to supply the obvious MOP multi-index
\[
(m_0,\ldots,m_{d-1}).
\]

But the stable twist does **not** choose one such vector.

It sums over every composition
\[
m_0+\cdots+m_{d-1}=m.
\]

Thus:
\[
\boxed{
\textbf{there is no single natural MOP multi-index attached to the full twist.}
}
\]

A type-II MOP \(P_{\mathbf n}\) is one polynomial associated with one \(\mathbf n\).

The twist is a superposition over all of them before one even asks about orthogonality.

---

# 8. Fixing the quotient sizes does not rescue independence

At
\[
n=9,\qquad d=3,
\]
fix the quotient-size composition
\[
(m_0,m_1,m_2)=(0,1,2).
\]

Two contributing shapes are

\[
\lambda=(6,3),
\qquad
(\nu_0,\nu_1,\nu_2)
=
(\varnothing,(1),(2)),
\]
with
\[
\varepsilon_3(\lambda)=+1,
\qquad
\frac{f^\lambda}{|\chi^\lambda(3^3)|}=16,
\]

while

\[
\lambda=(3,3,2,1),
\qquad
(\nu_0,\nu_1,\nu_2)
=
(\varnothing,(1),(1,1)),
\]
has
\[
\varepsilon_3(\lambda)=-1,
\qquad
\frac{f^\lambda}{|\chi^\lambda(3^3)|}=56.
\]

So at the **same quotient-size multi-index**:
\[
\boxed{
\varepsilon_d
\text{ can change sign}
}
\]
and
\[
\boxed{
\text{the dimension coupling changes magnitude}.
}
\]

Across the finite audit,
\[
\boxed{166}
\]
fixed-composition groups exhibit both \(d\)-signs, and
\[
\boxed{263}
\]
groups exhibit nonconstant full-dimension coupling.

---

# 9. The degree test also disagrees with the natural MOP index

For a type-II MOP with natural quotient multi-index,
\[
|\mathbf n|=m,
\]
the polynomial degree would be exactly
\[
m.
\]

The twist degree is instead controlled by the number of admissible \(2\)-core charges, hence only \(O(\sqrt n)\).

Across the audited nonidentity twists through \(n=45\),
\[
\boxed{25/62}
\]
have
\[
\deg\mathcal T_{n,d}\ne m.
\]

Some small layers coincide accidentally, so degree alone is not the fundamental obstruction.

The fundamental obstruction is still that the full quotient formula sums **all** compositions rather than selecting one multi-index.

---

# 10. The mixed determinant is a partition function, not yet an MOP polynomial

The 06L determinant
\[
\det K_{r,d}(t,u)
\]
is naturally a biorthogonal/tau **partition function**.

The variable
\[
u
\]
is an external fugacity counting BG/core charge.

In standard MOP theory the polynomial variable is the spectral/particle coordinate, and the orthogonality relations are imposed on that variable.

These roles are different.

So even though multiple orthogonal polynomials frequently arise from biorthogonal determinant ensembles, it does not follow that
\[
\mathcal T_{n,d}(u)
\]
is itself the associated type-I or type-II MOP.

An additional exact identity would be needed.

06N finds no such identity.

---

# 11. Why the 2026 normality theorem cannot yet be used

Kozhan and Vaktnäs provide powerful zero-location and interlacing criteria for genuine type-I and type-II multiple orthogonal polynomials in terms of normality of Christoffel-transformed systems.

But normality is not a property one can test before specifying:

1. a fixed measure system;
2. a fixed multi-index;
3. the identification of the target polynomial with the corresponding MOP.

For
\[
\mathcal T_{n,d}(u),
\]
06N does not obtain those objects.

Therefore:
\[
\boxed{
\textbf{the normality theorem is not currently applicable to the twist root problem.}
}
\]

This does not prove that no more sophisticated multiple-orthogonal representation exists.

It rejects the natural multiple-Charlier interpretation proposed after 06M.

---

# 12. Stabilization occurs only at the terminal rank

Every partition of \(n\) has length at most \(n\), so length rank
\[
r=n
\]
contains the complete Schur sum.

At
\[
r=n-1,
\]
the partition
\[
(1^n)
\]
is still omitted.

For
\[
n=dm,\qquad d\text{ odd},
\]
the class \(d^m\) is an even permutation, so the sign representation gives
\[
\chi^{(1^n)}(d^m)=+1.
\]

Also
\[
f^{(1^n)}=1.
\]

Thus the \(r=n-1\) coefficient differs from the full coefficient.

Hence:
\[
\boxed{
\textbf{the eventual full-Schur stabilization threshold is exactly }r=n.
}
\]

Direct finite length-cutoff calculations confirm the first full match at \(r=n\) in all
\[
\boxed{14/14}
\]
audited cases through \(n=15\).

---

# 13. Main verdict

### Littlewood \(d\)-character factorization
\[
\boxed{\textbf{PASS EXACTLY}}
\]

### Odd-\(d\) BG quotient factorization
\[
\boxed{\textbf{PASS EXACTLY}}
\]

### Stable signed quotient-convolution formula
\[
\boxed{\textbf{PASS EXACTLY}}
\]

### Standard multiple-Charlier/type-II identification
\[
\boxed{\textbf{REJECT}}
\]

### Positive independent quotient measures
\[
\boxed{\textbf{REJECT}}
\]

### MOP normality theorem applied to the twist
\[
\boxed{\textbf{NOT AVAILABLE}}
\]

### Full-Schur stabilization threshold
\[
\boxed{r=n\textbf{ EXACTLY}}
\]

### Full twist roots
\[
\boxed{\textbf{104/104 FINITE PASS THROUGH }n=45}
\]

### All-\(n\) twist theorem
\[
\boxed{\textbf{OPEN}}
\]

So the MOP route has done its job even though it did not prove the roots: it forced us to expose the actual stable structure.

The twist is best regarded not as a standard multiple-Charlier polynomial, but as
\[
\boxed{
\textbf{a signed, globally coupled \(d\)-quotient convolution with an additive BG charge.}
}
\]

---

# 14. Recommended next audit

## EHF-PHYS-ALG-06O — Coupled \(d\)-Quotient PF Kernel / \(2d\)-Abacus Transfer Audit

The target should now be
\[
\mathcal K_d(\boldsymbol\nu)
=
\varepsilon_d(\Lambda_d(\boldsymbol\nu))
f^{\Lambda_d(\boldsymbol\nu)}.
\]

Questions:

1. express \(\mathcal K_d\) completely in \(d\)-runner beta coordinates;
2. separate within-runner PF factors from the cross-runner Cauchy/Vandermonde factor;
3. combine the \(d\)-runner decomposition with parity/BG on one \(2d\)-abacus;
4. determine whether summing the cross-runner kernel defines a PF-preserving operator on products of quotient BG polynomials;
5. test whether the signed \(d\)-kernel is a determinant/minor of a larger totally-positive \(2d\)-runner network even though the grand mixed pencil was not sign-regular;
6. derive a transfer in the quotient charge vector before projecting to the alternating sum;
7. use \(d=1\) as the Bessel/PF base and \(d=3\) as the first coupled case.

This is now the most direct representation-theoretic route to the persistent finite twist real-root phenomenon.


---

# EHF-PHYS-ALG-06O — Coupled \(d\)-Quotient PF Kernel / \(2d\)-Abacus Transfer Audit

**Branch:** EHF-PHYS-ALG  
**Inputs:** PHYS-ALG-06N plus the signed-twist census of 06K/06L.  
**Primary target:** isolate the coupled \(d\)-quotient kernel exactly, combine quotient and BG parity on a \(2d\)-abacus, and determine whether the kernel induces a PF-preserving transfer on quotient charge data.

**Final status:** the coupled kernel is exactly a signed merge-Vandermonde/product-of-resultants, and it has an exact \(d\)-ribbon transfer ratio on the full \(2d\)-abacus microstate. A charge-only transfer is **not closed**: strong lumpability fails even after fixing quotient sizes and component BG charges. The simple PF-operator route is therefore rejected. A new paired finite phenomenon emerges instead: both the positive absolute-character core polynomial and the oriented/signed twist are real-rooted in all \(104\) full odd-divisor layers through \(n=45\).

**Certification:** \(\boxed{5749/5749}\) expected-outcome, exact finite, and scope checks pass.

---

## 1. Simultaneous \(d\)-quotient and parity coordinates

Let \(d\) be odd and let \(\lambda\) have empty \(d\)-core.

Choose
\[
r=ds
\]
beta beads, with \(s\) even.

Write the \(d\)-quotient as
\[
(\nu_0,\ldots,\nu_{d-1})
\]
and define
\[
X_a=\beta(\nu_a;s).
\]

Then
\[
\boxed{
\beta(\lambda;r)
=
\bigsqcup_{a=0}^{d-1}
\{a+dx:x\in X_a\}.
}
\]

This is the ordinary \(d\)-abacus.

Because \(d\) is odd,
\[
a+dx\pmod{2d}
=
\begin{cases}
a,&x\text{ even},\\
a+d,&x\text{ odd}.
\end{cases}
\]

Thus each \(d\)-runner splits canonically into two runners modulo \(2d\).

That is the desired \(2d\)-abacus.

---

## 2. BG charge is the \(2d\)-runner imbalance

For even \(s\),
\[
BG(\nu_a)
=
\frac{N_{\rm even}(X_a)-N_{\rm odd}(X_a)}2.
\]

The parity of the original beta bead is
\[
(-1)^{a+x}.
\]

Therefore
\[
\boxed{
BG(\lambda)
=
\sum_{a=0}^{d-1}
(-1)^aBG(\nu_a).
}
\]

So the Berkovich–Garvan quotient formula becomes visually immediate on the combined \(2d\)-abacus.

This identity was checked simultaneously with the kernel formula on every one of the \(1125\) quotient microstates in the main finite kernel census.

---

# 3. A quotient box is a \(d\)-ribbon move

Add one box of content
\[
c=j-i
\]
to quotient component \(\nu_a\).

In quotient beta coordinates, one bead moves
\[
x\longmapsto x+1.
\]

In the original beta set:
\[
\boxed{
b=a+dx
\longmapsto
b+d.
}
\]

So one quotient-box addition is exactly one \(d\)-ribbon addition to \(\lambda\).

Since moving \(x\) to \(x+1\) toggles its parity, the global BG change is
\[
\boxed{
\Delta BG
=
(-1)^{a+c}.
}
\]

Direct finite audit:
\[
\boxed{675/675}
\]
one-box moves reproduce this rule.

---

# 4. Normalize the Littlewood sign correctly

Let
\[
w_d(\lambda;r)
\]
be the permutation that sorts the beta set by increasing residue modulo \(d\), and decreasing beta value within each residue.

The ribbon/Littlewood sign relative to the empty core is
\[
\boxed{
\varepsilon_d(\lambda)
=
\operatorname{sgn}w_d(\lambda;r)\,
\operatorname{sgn}w_d(\varnothing;r).
}
\]

The empty-abacus factor is essential if the bead rank is changed.

Define
\[
\sigma_0(d,s)
=
\operatorname{sgn}w_d(\varnothing;ds).
\]

All formulas below use this normalized sign.

---

# 5. Sign and dimension collapse to one determinant

Let
\[
S
=
\big(
X_0,\,
1+dX_1,\,
\ldots,\,
d-1+dX_{d-1}
\big)
\]
denote the residue-grouped beta sequence explicitly:
first all residue-\(0\) beads in decreasing order, then residue-\(1\), and so on.

The ordinary beta hook formula is
\[
f^\lambda
=
n!\frac{|\Delta(\beta(\lambda;r))|}
{\prod_{b\in\beta(\lambda;r)}b!}.
\]

Permuting the descending beta sequence into residue-grouped order changes the Vandermonde sign by
\[
\operatorname{sgn}w_d(\lambda;r).
\]

Hence
\[
\boxed{
\varepsilon_d(\lambda)f^\lambda
=
\sigma_0(d,s)\,
n!\,
\frac{\Delta(S)}
{\displaystyle\prod_{a=0}^{d-1}\prod_{x\in X_a}(a+dx)!}.
}
\]

This is the principal structural identity of 06O.

The Littlewood sign is not an extra decoration on a positive dimension kernel.

It is the **orientation of the same Vandermonde determinant**.

---

# 6. Exact cross-runner factorization

The residue-grouped Vandermonde factors as
\[
\boxed{
\Delta(S)
=
d^{\,d\binom{s}{2}}
\left(
\prod_{a=0}^{d-1}\Delta(X_a)
\right)
C_d(\mathbf X),
}
\]
where
\[
\boxed{
C_d(\mathbf X)
=
\prod_{0\le a<b<d}
\prod_{\substack{x\in X_a\\y\in X_b}}
[d(x-y)+a-b].
}
\]

Using
\[
f^{\nu_a}
=
m_a!
\frac{\Delta(X_a)}{\prod_{x\in X_a}x!},
\qquad
m_a=|\nu_a|,
\]
gives
\[
\boxed{
\begin{aligned}
\varepsilon_d(\lambda)f^\lambda
={}&
\sigma_0(d,s)\,
n!\,
d^{\,d\binom{s}{2}}
C_d(\mathbf X)
\\
&\times
\prod_{a=0}^{d-1}
\left[
\frac{f^{\nu_a}}{m_a!}
\prod_{x\in X_a}
\frac{x!}{(a+dx)!}
\right].
\end{aligned}
}
\]

This was verified exactly over the complete finite microstate audit.

---

# 7. The full twist summand has squared quotient dimensions

Littlewood gives
\[
\chi^\lambda(d^m)
=
\varepsilon_d(\lambda)
\frac{m!}{\prod_am_a!}
\prod_af^{\nu_a}.
\]

Therefore the contribution
\[
f^\lambda\chi^\lambda(d^m)
\]
becomes, up to a constant depending only on \(n,d,s\),

\[
\boxed{
C_d(\mathbf X)
\prod_{a=0}^{d-1}
\left[
\frac{(f^{\nu_a})^2}{(m_a!)^2}
\prod_{x\in X_a}
\frac{x!}{(a+dx)!}
\right].
}
\]

This is an important refinement of 06N.

Every quotient-local Specht factor is now **squared**, giving it a Plancherel-like form.

All signed nonlocal dependence is concentrated in
\[
C_d(\mathbf X).
\]

---

# 8. The cross kernel is a product of shifted resultants

Define
\[
P_a(z)
=
\prod_{x\in X_a}(z-x).
\]

For \(a<b\),
\[
\begin{aligned}
&\prod_{x\in X_a,y\in X_b}
[d(x-y)+a-b]
\\[1mm]
&\qquad=
d^{s^2}
\prod_{x,y}
\left[
x-y+\frac{a-b}{d}
\right]
\\
&\qquad=
\boxed{
d^{s^2}
\operatorname{Res}
\left(
P_a(z),
P_b\!\left(z+\frac{a-b}{d}\right)
\right).
}
\end{aligned}
\]

Hence
\[
\boxed{
C_d(\mathbf X)
=
d^{s^2\binom d2}
\prod_{a<b}
\operatorname{Res}
\left(
P_a(z),
P_b\!\left(z+\frac{a-b}{d}\right)
\right).
}
\]

PHYS-ALG-06O verified the resultant identity exactly on \(400\) representative configurations.

Equivalently, put
\[
\xi_{a,x}=x+\frac ad.
\]

Then
\[
a+dx=d\xi_{a,x},
\]
and the complete residue-grouped coupling is simply the Vandermonde determinant of the merged shifted nodes
\[
\{\xi_{a,x}\}.
\]

So the kernel is a **merge determinant**.

---

# 9. The sign is a runner-shuffle orientation

Within each runner,
\[
x_1>x_2>\cdots
\]
is already ordered.

The sign changes arise only when the \(d\) individually sorted runner lists are merged into their global numerical beta order.

Thus the Littlewood sign can be read as the parity of that merge/shuffle, relative to the empty-abacus baseline.

This explains a recurring feature of the finite data:

\[
\boxed{
\textbf{the sign and the cross-runner magnitude should not be separated.}
}
\]

They are the orientation and modulus of the same determinant.

---

# 10. Exact microscopic \(d\)-ribbon transfer

Suppose a quotient bead
\[
x\in X_a
\]
moves to
\[
x+1.
\]

The corresponding original beta bead is
\[
b=a+dx
\]
and moves to
\[
b+d.
\]

Let
\[
\mathcal K(\lambda)
=
\varepsilon_d(\lambda)f^\lambda.
\]

Then
\[
\boxed{
\frac{\mathcal K(\lambda')}{\mathcal K(\lambda)}
=
\frac{(n+d)!}{n!}
\frac{b!}{(b+d)!}
\prod_{\substack{b'\in B\\b'\ne b}}
\frac{b+d-b'}{b-b'}.
}
\]

This follows immediately from the signed Vandermonde formula.

The entire \(d\)-runner interaction appears explicitly in the product over all other beta beads.

The finite transfer census validates the formula exactly.

---

# 11. Transfer ratio for the complete twist term

Let
\[
W(\boldsymbol\nu)
=
\varepsilon_d(\lambda)f^\lambda
\;
m!\prod_a\frac{f^{\nu_a}}{m_a!}.
\]

Adding one box to \(\nu_a\) gives
\[
\boxed{
\frac{W(\boldsymbol\nu')}{W(\boldsymbol\nu)}
=
\frac{\mathcal K(\lambda')}{\mathcal K(\lambda)}
\,
\frac{m+1}{m_a+1}
\,
\frac{f^{\nu_a'}}{f^{\nu_a}}.
}
\]

Thus 06O does obtain an exact microscopic transfer.

Because the transfer is a ratio of a state function, independent additions satisfy a multiplicative zero-curvature/cocycle relation automatically.

But that does **not** mean the transfer closes on the BG charges.

---

# 12. Charge-vector lumpability fails

Suppose we try to discard the actual quotient shapes and retain only

\[
(m_0,\ldots,m_{d-1})
\]
and
\[
(BG(\nu_0),\ldots,BG(\nu_{d-1})).
\]

For
\[
d=3,\qquad m=2,
\]
consider
\[
(\varnothing,\varnothing,(2))
\]
and
\[
(\varnothing,\varnothing,(1,1)).
\]

Both have
\[
(m_0,m_1,m_2)=(0,0,2)
\]
and
\[
(BG(\nu_0),BG(\nu_1),BG(\nu_2))
=
(0,0,0).
\]

Yet their aggregate signed full-term transfer ratios by global charge change are:

\[
(\varnothing,\varnothing,(2)):
\qquad
\Delta BG=+1:\ -314,
\qquad
\Delta BG=-1:\ 256,
\]

whereas

\[
(\varnothing,\varnothing,(1,1)):
\qquad
\Delta BG=+1:\ 28,
\qquad
\Delta BG=-1:\ -\frac{196}{5}.
\]

Therefore
\[
\boxed{
\textbf{the transfer is not strongly lumpable to the quotient charge vector.}
}
\]

The detailed beta geometry is essential.

---

# 13. Even the positive transfer does not lump

Take absolute full-term weights instead.

For the same two states the outgoing positive ratios are

\[
(\varnothing,\varnothing,(2)):
\qquad
(+1:316,\ -1:256),
\]

and

\[
(\varnothing,\varnothing,(1,1)):
\qquad
\left(+1:28,\ -1:\frac{308}{5}\right).
\]

They still differ.

So the obstruction is not merely caused by signs.

There is no natural state-independent PF transfer matrix whose state is only the size/BG vector.

This closes the most naive version of the proposed \(2d\)-abacus PF kernel.

---

# 14. Dissecting the coupled determinant

06O separated the full summand into four diagnostic variants.

### A. Local positive factors only

Set
\[
C_d(\mathbf X)\to1.
\]

This is almost—but not universally—PF in the finite audit.

At
\[
d=5,\qquad m=6,
\]
the local-only core polynomial has nonreal roots.

So:
\[
\boxed{
\textbf{the quotient-local product alone does not explain the theorem.}
}
\]

### B. Keep only the sign of \(C_d\)

Replace
\[
C_d\to\operatorname{sgn}(C_d).
\]

This fails earlier.

At
\[
d=3,\qquad m=5,
\]
the primitive polynomial is
\[
-3997839103258000x^2
-
7881382359513181x
-
6850659488663820,
\]
whose discriminant is
\[
\boxed{
-47435149650697143619703589501239<0.
}
\]

Thus:
\[
\boxed{
\textbf{the Littlewood/merge sign by itself destroys real-rootedness.}
}
\]

### C. Absolute cross kernel

Use
\[
|C_d(\mathbf X)|.
\]

All \(16/16\) finite quotient-level test cases are positive with only negative real roots.

### D. Signed full cross kernel

Use the actual
\[
C_d(\mathbf X).
\]

All \(16/16\) tested cases are real-rooted.

So the cross-runner **magnitude** is doing genuine restorative work.

---

# 15. New positive polynomial: absolute-character core PF

The absolute-cross variant has a simple representation-theoretic meaning.

Define
\[
\boxed{
A_{n,d}(x)
=
\sum_q
\left[
\sum_{\substack{\lambda\vdash n\\
\core_2(\lambda)=\kappa(q)}}
f^\lambda
\left|
\chi^\lambda(d^{n/d})
\right|
\right]
x^{j(q)}.
}
\]

All coefficients are nonnegative.

At
\[
d=1,
\]
this is exactly the Plancherel BG polynomial because
\[
|\chi^\lambda(1^n)|=f^\lambda.
\]

06O regenerated \(A_{n,d}\) for **every odd divisor** in the existing full range
\[
4\le n\le45.
\]

Exact Sturm result:
\[
\boxed{
104/104
\text{ absolute-character core polynomials have only negative real roots.}
}
\]

This is a new finite result.

---

# 16. Ribbon-tableau interpretation

For empty \(d\)-core,
\[
|\chi^\lambda(d^m)|
=
\frac{m!}{\prod_am_a!}
\prod_af^{\nu_a}.
\]

This is also the natural count of standard \(d\)-ribbon tableaux of shape \(\lambda\) under the Stanton–White quotient correspondence, while
\[
\varepsilon_d(\lambda)
\]
is the common ribbon sign.

Therefore
\[
f^\lambda|\chi^\lambda(d^m)|
\]
counts pairs consisting of:

- a standard Young tableau of shape \(\lambda\);
- a standard \(d\)-ribbon tableau of the same shape.

So \(A_{n,d}\) is a **positive pair-enumeration by BG/core charge**.

The ordinary twist replaces the ribbon count by its common orientation sign.

This gives the paired structure:

\[
\boxed{
\begin{array}{c}
\text{absolute ribbon count}
\longrightarrow
A_{n,d}(x)
\quad\text{PF in }104/104,
\\[2mm]
\text{oriented ribbon count}
\longrightarrow
\mathcal T_{n,d}(x)
\quad\text{real-rooted in }104/104.
\end{array}
}
\]

That may be the cleanest formulation of the remaining theorem problem.

---

# 17. An uncoupled BG signal also appears

Define
\[
I_n(x)
=
\sum_{\lambda\vdash n}
f^\lambda x^{j(BG(\lambda))}.
\]

Because
\[
\sum_\lambda f^\lambda
\]
counts involutions under RSK, this is the BG/core distribution of involution shapes.

Exact finite census:
\[
\boxed{
I_n(x)
\text{ has only negative real roots for every }0\le n\le30.
}
\]

So the uncoupled quotient components themselves appear to carry another PF family.

This helps explain why the local product is so often close to PF, but the \(d=5,m=6\) counterexample proves that fixed-total coupling of those components is not automatically enough.

---

# 18. Why Borcea–Brändén is not yet the answer

Real-rootedness-preserving linear operators can be attacked via their stability symbols.

But the microscopic \(d\)-ribbon transfer has now been shown not to lump to a state-independent operator on the charge polynomial.

So there is currently no canonical linear map
\[
T_d:
\text{charge polynomial at }m
\longmapsto
\text{charge polynomial at }m+1
\]
whose Borcea–Brändén symbol we can test.

One could enlarge the state space to retain the full quotient beta configuration.

That may eventually produce a multivariate stability problem.

But the simple one-variable charge-only operator does not exist.

---

# 19. Main verdict

### \(2d\)-abacus
\[
\boxed{\textbf{PASS EXACTLY}}
\]

### Littlewood sign + dimension as one signed Vandermonde
\[
\boxed{\textbf{PASS EXACTLY}}
\]

### Shifted-resultant kernel
\[
\boxed{\textbf{PASS EXACTLY}}
\]

### Exact microscopic \(d\)-ribbon transfer
\[
\boxed{\textbf{PASS EXACTLY}}
\]

### Charge-vector closed transfer
\[
\boxed{\textbf{FAIL}}
\]

### Sign-only PF action
\[
\boxed{\textbf{FAIL}}
\]

### Local-only PF action
\[
\boxed{\textbf{FAIL}}
\]

### Absolute-character positive polynomial
\[
\boxed{
\textbf{104/104 NEGATIVE-REAL-ROOTED THROUGH }n=45
}
\]

### Signed twist
\[
\boxed{
\textbf{104/104 REAL-ROOTED THROUGH }n=45
}
\]

### All-\(n\) theorem
\[
\boxed{\textbf{OPEN}}
\]

PHYS-ALG-06O therefore replaces the hoped-for “PF transfer on core charge” with a more precise object:

\[
\boxed{
\textbf{a full microstate merge/resultant determinant whose absolute and oriented stable sums both exhibit exceptional root structure.}
}
\]

---

# 20. Recommended next audit

## EHF-PHYS-ALG-06P — Absolute-Character Ribbon-Tableau PF / Orientation Deformation Audit

The positive polynomial
\[
A_{n,d}(x)
=
\sum_{\lambda}
f^\lambda|\chi^\lambda(d^{n/d})|
x^{j(BG(\lambda))}
\]
is now the cleanest theorem target.

Targets:

1. express \(A_{n,d}\) directly as a count of pairs
   \[
   (\text{SYT},\text{standard }d\text{-ribbon tableau})
   \]
   by BG/core charge;

2. use Stanton–White to convert the ribbon tableau into a \(d\)-tuple of SYT and seek an RSK/matching model for the **absolute** merge kernel;

3. determine whether \(A_{n,d}\) is a determinantal or strongly-Rayleigh marginal even though the signed grand pencil is not;

4. test for a recurrence or stable-polynomial lift of the positive \(104/104\) family;

5. only after the positive theorem is understood, reintroduce the shuffle orientation
   \[
   \varepsilon_d
   \]
   and determine why it sends the positive PF polynomial to another real-rooted polynomial;

6. investigate the newly discovered involution-BG family
   \[
   I_n(x)=\sum_\lambda f^\lambda x^{j(BG(\lambda))}
   \]
   as the \(d\)-quotient component building block.

The absolute-character family removes the signed-character complication while retaining the full cross-runner magnitude. It therefore looks like the best current route to an all-\(n\) theorem.
